Introduction to Cloud Computing 

`What is cloud computing ?` Cloud computing is the delivery of computing services over the internet, enabling flexible resources, faster innovation, and economies of scale. It allows users to access and store data remotely rather than on local servers or personal computers.Cloud computing overs services with pay as you go pricing model instead of buying , owning and maintaining physical data centers and servers .

`Benefits of Cloud Computing `

1. **Scalability and Flexibity in cloud computing**: Cloud computing allows businesses to easily scale (increase or decrease) their resources up or down based on demand, ensuring they only pay for what they use.
+ Companies can Instantly deploy applications and services to data centers worldwide.
Easily accommodate business expansion or new ventures without significant infrastructure changes.

data residency: 
Cloud providers often have data centers in multiple regions, allowing businesses to choose where their data is stored to comply with local regulations and improve performance for users in specific locations.

2. **Cost Efficiency**: Cloud computing eliminates the need for upfront capital expenditures on hardware and infrastructure. Businesses can pay for cloud services on a subscription or pay-as-you-go basis, reducing operational costs and allowing for better budget management.
+ You dont need to spend money on physical data center e,g renting or buying in other manage your I.T Equipments.You can do this over the internet just by having a computer .
+ It eliminates need of managing physical hardware ,data centers and maintenance
+ No need to power and cool on premise servers , saving electricity cost .

3. **Reliability and High Availability**: Cloud providers typically offer robust infrastructure with built-in redundancy and failover mechanisms, ensuring high availability and minimizing downtime for applications and services.
+ Cloud providers often have multiple data centers across different regions, providing redundancy and failover capabilities to
ensure high availability and minimize downtime for applications and services.

4. **Security**: Cloud providers invest heavily in security measures to protect their infrastructure and customer data. They often have dedicated security teams, advanced encryption techniques, and compliance certifications to ensure the safety of data stored in the cloud.
+ Advanced Threat Protection: Leading providers invest in robust cybersecurity measures, including encryption, firewalls, and intrusion detection systems.
+ Compliance: Providers adhere to strict regulatory standards (e.g., GDPR, HIPAA, SOC) to protect sensitive data.
Shared Responsibility Model: Users retain control over access, while the provider secures infrastructure.
+ Maintenance and Upgrades are easy :Most of the time the service provider is responsible for maintaining , managing and updating the infrastructure . This reliefs businesses from system maintenance , allows them to focus on the main activities .

5. **Global Reach**: Cloud providers have data centers located around the world, allowing businesses to deploy applications and services closer to their users, improving performance and reducing latency.

6. **Disaster Recovery and Business Continuity**
Cloud providers often offer backup and disaster recovery solutions, allowing businesses to quickly recover data and applications in the event of a disaster or outage.
+ Automated Backups: Built-in options for reliable data backup and recovery
+ High Availability: Redundant systems minimize downtime and provide 24/7 service.
+ Global Redundancy: Data replication across multiple regions ensures resilience against localized failures.

7. **Innovation and Agility**: Cloud computing enables businesses to quickly deploy and experiment with new technologies and services, fostering innovation and agility in responding to market changes and customer needs.

8. **Accessibility And Monitoring**
Cloud services can be accessed from anywhere with an internet connection, facilitating remote work and collaboration.


**AMAZON WEB SERVICES** AWS 

Amazon Web Services (AWS) is a comprehensive cloud computing platform offered by Amazon, providing over 200 fully featured services from global data centers.These services include computing power, database storage, content delivery, and various other functionalities that enable businesses and developers to scale and innovate efficiently.

Launched in 2002, AWS has become a leading provider in the cloud computing industry, serving millions of customers worldwide, including startups, large enterprises, and government agencies. Its offerings are designed to be flexible and secure, meeting the requirements of organizations with high-sensitivity needs, such as the military and global banks.

AWS operates on a pay-as-you-go pricing model, allowing users to access resources as needed without upfront capital expenses. This approach facilitates cost-effective scalability and agility, empowering users to experiment and innovate more rapidly.

`on premise` : build their own I.T infrastructures (data centres ) 


**Deployment Models in Cloud Computing**

`What is a deployment Model ?`

A deployment model in cloud computing refers to the specific configuration and arrangement of cloud resources and services that an organization uses to deliver its applications and services. It defines how the cloud infrastructure is set up, who has access to it, and how it is managed. The choice of deployment model depends on factors such as `security requirements, compliance needs, budget constraints, and the level of control desired by the organization`.


`Public Cloud` :

The public cloud is a deployment model in which cloud computing services are delivered over the internet by third-party providers. These services, such as computing power, storage, and applications, are shared among multiple users (known as "tenants") and are accessible to the general public.The provider owns, manages, and assumes all responsibility for the data centers and infrastructure. Examples include `Amazon Web Services (AWS),` `Microsoft Azure`, and `Google Cloud Platform (GCP)`.

`Private Cloud`

The private cloud is a cloud deployment model in which computing resources are used exclusively by a single organization. These resources can be hosted on-premises (within the organization's data center) or by a third-party provider but remain dedicated solely to that organization, ensuring greater control, privacy, and security.

`Hybrid Cloud`

The hybrid cloud is a cloud computing model that combines the use of both public and private cloud environments, allowing data and applications to be shared between them. This approach enables businesses to leverage the scalability and cost-efficiency of public cloud services while maintaining the security and control of private cloud infrastructure.




**SERVICE MODELS**

`What is a service model ?`
A service model in cloud computing refers to the specific type of service that a cloud provider offers to   
users. It defines the level of abstraction and control that users have over the underlying infrastructure and resources. The three primary service models in cloud computing are `Infrastructure as a Service (IaaS)`, `Platform as a Service (PaaS)`, and  `Software as a Service (SaaS)`.

1. `Infrastructure as a Service (Iaas)`

Infrastructure as a Service (IaaS) is a cloud computing service model that provides virtualized computing resources over the internet. In this model, users can rent or lease virtual machines, storage, and networking resources from a cloud provider on a pay-as-you-go basis. IaaS allows users to have control over the operating systems, applications, and data while the cloud provider manages the underlying infrastructure.

2. `Platform as a Service (PaaS)`
Platform as a Service (PaaS) is a cloud computing service model that provides a platform for developers to build, deploy, and manage applications without worrying about the underlying infrastructure. PaaS offers a complete development environment, including tools, libraries, and runtime environments, allowing developers to focus on coding and application logic rather than infrastructure management.

3. `Software as a Service (SaaS)`

Software as a Service (SaaS) is a cloud computing service model that delivers software applications over the internet on a subscription basis. In this model, users can access and use software applications hosted by a cloud provider without needing to install or maintain them on their local devices. SaaS applications are typically accessed through web browsers, and the cloud provider is responsible for managing the infrastructure, security, and updates of the software.



cloud providers now also offers 

`Infrastructure as code(IAC)` : This is a service where you can deploy your infrastructure using code .
+ `Function as a Service (FaaS)`: A serverless computing model that allows developers to run code in response to events without managing servers.
+ `Container as a Service (CaaS)`: A cloud service model that provides container
orchestration and management, allowing users to deploy and manage containerized applications.   
+ `Database as a Service (DBaaS)`: A cloud service model that provides managed database services, allowing users to access and manage databases without the need for infrastructure management.
+ `Storage as a Service (STaaS)`: A cloud service model that provides scalable and on-demand storage solutions, allowing users to store and access data without managing physical storage infrastructure.
+ `Artificial Intelligence as a Service (AIaaS)`: A cloud service model that provides AI and machine learning capabilities as a service, allowing users to integrate AI functionalities into their applications without needing to build and maintain their own AI infrastructure.


**What is a Data Center** 

A data center is a centralized facility or a Physical location used by organizations or company to house/keep and manage their IT infrastructures, including servers, storage systems, networking equipment, and other critical components for storing, processing, and disseminating(distrubuting) data and applications

AWS has its own data centers and they offer the cloud services over the internet from this phyiscal locations . customers , companies ,organisations store their data in this physical locations and they refer to their data centers as `AVAILABILITY ZONES` (AZ)


**What is a Region**

In Amazon Web Services (AWS), a region is a geographical area where AWS has multiple data centers (availability zones). Each region is designed to be completely isolated from other regions to ensure fault tolerance and stability. AWS operates multiple regions globally, and each region consists of multiple availability zones.

+ Each region is identified by a unique name (e.g., us-east-1, eu-west-1 , ca-central-1 ).
+ Regions are composed of multiple availability zones (data centres).
+ AWS services are typically deployed globally across multiple regions to provide redundancy and improve performance for users located in different geographic locations.



**WHAT IS AN AVAILABILITY ZONE (AZ)**

An availability zone (AZ) is one or more discrete data centers within a region. These availability zones are interconnected through low-latency links, but they are physically separated from each other. This physical separation is important for providing redundancy and resilience. If one availability zone within a region experiences a failure, the other availability zones in the same region can continue to operate independently, ensuring high availability and fault tolerance for applications and services hosted on AWS.

+ Availability zones are isolated from each other to minimize the impact of failures and improve fault tolerance.
+ They are connected through high-speed, low-latency links to enable synchronous replication and data transfer between availability zones.
+ Deploying applications across multiple availability zones within a region enhances fault tolerance and high availability by ensuring that failures in one availability zone do not impact the availability of applications hosted in other availability zones.


**Why did AWS Introduce Multi Availability Zones ? (AZ)** 

+ `Disaster Recovery` : 

Availability zones facilitate disaster recovery by providing geographically separated infrastructure within the same region. Organizations can use availability zones to replicate data and resources across different zones, enabling them to quickly recover from disasters or regional outages without data loss or downtime.

+ `Improved Perfomance` :

Availability zones are interconnected through low-latency links within the same region. This allows for synchronous data replication and enables applications to achieve low-latency communication between components deployed across different availability zones, enhancing overall performance.

+ `High Availability`:

Applications deployed across multiple availability zones can achieve higher levels of availability because they are not dependent on a single data center or infrastructure. Even if one availability zone becomes unavailable due to maintenance, hardware failures, or other issues, the application can remain accessible from other availability zones.

+ `Fault Tolerance`

By distributing resources across multiple availability zones within the same region, applications can withstand failures in one availability zone without impacting availability. This redundancy ensures that if one availability zone experiences an outage, services can continue to operate from other availability zones.

+ `Data Redundancy`
Availability zones enable data redundancy by allowing organizations to replicate data across multiple zones within the same region. This ensures that even if one availability zone experiences a failure, the data remains intact and accessible from other availability zones, reducing the risk of data loss.

+ `Scalability and Flexibility`

Availability zones provide scalability by allowing organizations to distribute workloads across multiple zones within a region. As demand for resources grows, organizations can easily scale their applications horizontally by adding more instances or services across different availability zones

Horizontal scaling 
vertical scaling 

+ `Compliance and Data residency `

Some regulatory requirements mandate data residency within specific geographic regions. Availability zones allow organizations / companies to ensure compliance with these requirements by deploying resources in designated regions while still benefiting from the redundancy and fault tolerance provided by multiple availability zones.



**Virtualization**

`What is Virtualization` : Cloud providers like aws , azure , gcp are using the technology called Virtulization to offer I.T services over the internet. Virtualization in AWS (Amazon Web Services) or cloud computing refers to the practice of creating virtual instances of computing resources such as servers, storage, networks, and applications, which are then delivered over the internet. This allows users to access and utilize these resources on-demand without the need for physical hardware infrastructure.

In AWS, virtualization is achieved through technologies like Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (EC2) (computers or servers you can create on aws) for virtual servers, Amazon Simple Storage Service (S3) for virtual storage, and Amazon Virtual Private Cloud (VPC) for virtual networks. These services abstract the underlying hardware and provide users with scalable and flexible computing resources that can be easily provisioned and managed via a web interface or APIs.

Virtual Machine (VMs): A VM is a software-based emulation or imitation of a physical computer that runs its own operating system and applications. Virtual Machines are created and managed by the `hypervisor` and can be provisioned, cloned, migrated, and scaled as needed.

AWS has different services and it has categorized them based on scope or reach within the aws ecosystem or infrastructure

1. **Global Services** : These services are available globally and can be accessed from any AWS region. Examples include `Amazon Route 53` (DNS service) and `AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM)`.These are services that operates across multiple aws regions 

+ They are designed to provide a unified view and functionality regardless of the geographic region.
+ Examples of these services in aws IAM (Identity and access management),Route53 , Aws cloudfront , S3 buckets

2. **Regional Services** : These services are specific to a particular AWS region and are only available within that region. Examples include `Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (EC2)` and `Amazon Relational Database Service (RDS)`. These are services that operates within a specific aws region

They are confied to a specific geographical area of a single AWS region which may contain Multiple availability zones exmples of regional services can be VPC, Amazon EC2 , Amazon RDS , AWS lambda function , SNS etc 
*Usage: These services are used when data residency and latency requirements are tied to a specific geographic location.

3.**Zonal Services**

These services are specific to a particular availability zone within an AWS region. Examples include `Amazon Elastic Block Store (EBS)` and `Amazon Elastic File System (EFS)`. These are services that operates within a specific availability zone (data centre) of an aws region

+ They are limited to a single availability zone, providing high availability and fault isolation.

| Service Type    | Scope                          | Examples                                                      |
|-----------------|-------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Regional**    | Operate within a single region | Amazon VPC, Amazon EC2, Amazon RDS, AWS Lambda                |
| **Global**      | Operate across multiple regions| Amazon Route 53, AWS CloudFront, AWS IAM, Amazon S3 (global)  |
| **Zonal**       | Operate within a single AZ     | Amazon EC2 instances, Amazon EBS volumes                      |

`What is IAM ?` 

AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) is a web service that helps you securely control access to AWS resources. It allows you to manage users, groups, and permissions to AWS services and resources. With IAM, you can create and manage AWS users and groups, and use permissions to allow or deny their access to AWS resources. 

+ User Management 
+ Group Management 
+ Role Management
+ Policy Management
+ Multi-Factor Authentication (MFA)

Authentication ---> Password to login , Access keys 
Authorization --> Once user login what can they do ? 


IAM is a service that helps us to manage how users , groups , other services how they interact with our aws account and the resources in that account 


A). **ACCESS MANAGEMENT** ( `Users , User groups , Roles , Policies , Account Settings , Root Access Management , Identity Providers` )

`User Management` : A user in aws is anyone that can access or manage your aws resources .For a user to manage or access your aws resources you will need to provide them permission . A user can manage these resources through 2 ways ( GUI or CLI ). In aws a user or group of users can access the aws GUI using the `aws console` or a user can use the CLI or the user can use both  (`Authenticate and Authorization` )

+ Console (Graphical) (IAM USER)
+ CLI ( Commandline interface ) :

How to create a user and Grant them `AWS console access` which is GUI ?

1. Sign in to the AWS Management Console and open the IAM console at https://console.aws.amazon.com/iam/.
2. In the navigation pane, click on "Users" and then click on the "Add user"
3. Enter the user name for the new user and select the "AWS Management Console access" checkbox.
4. Set a custom password for the user or let AWS generate one automatically. You can also 
require the user to reset their password upon first login.
5. Click "Next: Permissions" to proceed to the permissions page.
6. On the "Set permissions" page, you can choose to add the user to an
existing group, copy permissions from an existing user, or attach policies directly to the user. Select the appropriate option based on your requirements and click "Next: Tags".  in our case we didnt give the user any permission yet 
7. Review the user details and permissions, and if everything looks correct, click "Create user
8. After the user is created, you will see a confirmation page with the user's access details. You can download the user's security credentials (including the password) or send them an email with the login instructions.



**How to create a user and Grant them `CLI access` ?**

**Step 1** 

1. Sign in to the AWS Management Console and open the IAM console at https://console.aws.amazon.com/iam/.
2. In the navigation pane, click on "Users" and then click on the "Add user"
3. Enter the user name for the new user and select the "Programmatic access" checkbox
4. Click "Next: Permissions" to proceed to the permissions page.
5. On the "Set permissions" page, you can choose to add the user to an 
existing group, copy permissions from an existing user, or attach policies directly to the user. Select the appropriate option based on your requirements and click "Next: Tags". `We skipped this permission part in our demo` 
6. Review the user details and permissions, and if everything looks correct, click "Create user"    
7. After the user is created, you will see a confirmation page with the user's access details. You can download the user's security credentials (including the access key ID and secret access key) or send them an email with the login instructions.

**Step 2**

1. Install the AWS Command Line Interface (CLI) on your local machine if you haven't already. You can download it from https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cli/latest/userguide/getting-started-install.html select your operating system ( windows , Mac , Linux )

2. Open a terminal or command prompt and run the following command to configure the AWS CLI 
3. Once CLI is completed configure aws cli using the following commands 

```bash
aws configure
```
4. You will be prompted to enter the following information:
+ AWS Access Key ID: Enter the access key ID that you obtained when you created the user
+ AWS Secret Access Key: Enter the secret access key that you obtained when you created the user
+ Default region name: Enter the default region you want to use (e.g., us-east)
+ Default output format: Enter the default output format (e.g., json, text, or table)

`Security best practise in IAM USER management` 

+ When you a create an aws account which comes with root user , avoid using the root instead create an IAM user and give the user specific permissions 
+ Every 90 days you want to rotate the access keys this prevent compromised key usage 
+ Manage users using groups as much as possible 
+ Regular Permission Audit (dormant , unused keys every quaterly  )
+ Use as much as `Customer managed policies` in production environments because it will follow least previlege access . 


1. `User Groups`

A user group in AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) is a collection of IAM users that can be managed as a single entity. User groups allow you to assign permissions to multiple users at once, making it easier to manage access to AWS resources. When you add a user to a group, the user inherits the permissions assigned to that group. This simplifies permission management and ensures consistent access control across multiple users who require similar permissions.

+ Cross functional : DevOPs ,Cloud Engineer , System Admin , Database admin , Cloud security engineers , Q.A , UI/UX . Software developers 
+ its a good practise to assigned permission to users in groups as much as possible .
+ When creating a group you can add an existing user while creating the new group or you can create a group without any user
+ When creating a group you can attach permission to the group . By default all users added to the group will automatically inherit the permission 
+ A single user can be in mulitple groups and by default the user will have permissions for all the groups
+ You can attach more than 1 policy to a group or a user ( a policy is a way to authorized what a user can do after they access your aws account )



**Benefits of Managing Permissions using Groups instead of assigning the permissions to individual users**

+ `It simplifies permission management` : Managing permissions through groups allows you to assign permissions to multiple users at once, reducing the administrative overhead of managing individual user permissions.
+ Assigning users to groups and attaching a permission to the group makes sure there is consistency across all users.All users wil have the same permissions unlike if i wanted to manually assign permissions to users you could make a mistake or forget to grant a user a required permission 
+ `It promotes consistency` : By using groups, you can ensure that users with similar roles and responsibilities have consistent permissions, reducing the risk of errors or omissions in permission assignments.
+ `It enhances security` : Managing permissions through groups allows you to implement the `principle of least privilege` more effectively. You can create groups with specific permissions based on job functions or roles, ensuring that users only have access to the resources they need to perform their tasks.
+ `It enables easier auditing and compliance` : Using groups makes it easier to track and audit permissions, as you can quickly see which users belong to which groups and what permissions are associated with each group. This can help with compliance requirements and security audits.

+`Managing users using `Groups` makes it Scalable`
Its easy to Onboard ,When a new user is added to the company adding them to the group speeds up their onboarding process
easy to offboarding when someone is leaving removing them from the group revokes their permission



2.  **Policies ( IAM POLICIES)**

`What is an IAM policy ?`

An IAM policy in AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) is a JSON document that defines permissions for an IAM user, group, or role. It specifies what actions are allowed or denied on specific AWS resources. IAM policies are used to control access to AWS services and resources, ensuring that users have the appropriate permissions to perform their tasks while adhering to the principle of least privilege. 


These are simply permissions you can grant to users or groups to carryout task or thier job functions (manage your aws resources) in your aws account e.g permission to create ec2 , or s3 bucket 


+ When you create an AWS account it comes with default policies and these policies are refered to as `AWS MANAGED POLICIES` 
+ Users can also create their own IAM policies . When user creates his or her own custom IAM policies its refered to as `CUSTOMER MANAGED POLICY` 

**WHAT IS A MANAGED POLICY ?** 

+ A Managed Policy is a standalone IAM policy that you can attach to multiple users, groups, or roles.

**There Two Types of Managed Policies**

1. `AWS Managed Policies`:

These are pre-defined policies created and maintained by AWS. They are designed to provide permissions for common use cases and are regularly updated by AWS to reflect changes in services and best practices. Examples include `AmazonS3ReadOnlyAccess` and `AmazonEC2FullAccess`.

+ These are policies that comes with your aws account by default and aws created policies and maintained these policies
e.g AdministratorAccess

+ To differentiate them on the console you will see the name `AWS managed` beside the policy 


`Benefits or advantages ` 

+ AWS Managed Policies are maintained and updated by AWS, ensuring that they are always up-to-date with the latest security best practices and service changes.
+ They provide a convenient way to grant permissions for common use cases without the need to create custom policy 
+ Pre-built, easy to use.


`Disadvantages of aws managed policies`

+ They may grant more permissions than necessary for specific use cases, which can lead to security risks if not carefully reviewed and monitored.
+ Too broad for production workloads and not really good for least previllage access
+ You `can not` edit or modify the policy . you can only attach to a user , group or roles


2. `Customer Managed Policies`:

These are policies that you create and manage yourself. Customer Managed Policies allow you to define custom permissions tailored to your specific needs and requirements. You have full control over the content of these policies, and you can modify them as needed.
+ You can attached the policy to multiple users and groups or role

`Benefits or advantages `

+ They allow you to create custom permissions that are tailored to your specific use cases and security requirements.
+ You have full control over the content of the policy and can modify it as needed to adapt to changing requirements or security best practices.
+ They are ideal for production workloads where you want to implement the principle of least privilege by granting only the permissions necessary for specific tasks or roles.

`Disadvantages of customer managed policies`

+ They require more effort and expertise to create and manage compared to AWS Managed Policies, as you need to define the permissions accurately to avoid security risks.
+ If not properly managed, they can lead to permission sprawl and security risks if too many policies are created or if policies are not regularly reviewed and updated.
+ They may require ongoing maintenance to ensure that they remain aligned with changing security best practices and service updates, which can be time-consuming for large and complex environments.

Apart from managed policies where we have (customer and aws managed policies) we also have an Inline Policy a user can create

**Inline Policy**

An inline policy is a type of IAM policy that is directly embedded within a specific IAM user, group, or role. Unlike managed policies, which are standalone and can be attached to multiple entities, inline policies are unique to the entity they are associated with and cannot be reused across different users, groups, or roles.
+ This is a policy created by the iam user and managed by the user
+ Unlike Managed Policies (standalone + reusable), inline policies are one-to-one with the entity (user , group or iam role).
+ If you delete the user/group/role, the inline policy goes too.
+ Not reusable: Cannot be shared with another identity.
+ Good for exceptions: Special permissions unique to one user/role.


`When to use an Inline Policy ?`

+ When you have a specific use case that requires permissions that are unique to a single user, group, or role and are not applicable to other entities.
+ When you want to ensure that the permissions defined in the policy are tightly coupled with the specific user, group, or role and should not be reused or shared across multiple entities.
+ When you want to create a policy that is only relevant to a specific user, group, or role and does not need to be maintained or updated independently of that entity.
+ When you have a temporary or one-time permission requirement that does not warrant the creation of a reusable managed policy.


**DIFFERENCE BETWEEN MANAGED POLICES AND INLINE POLICIES**

| Feature                   | AWS Managed Policies                    | Customer Managed Policies               | Inline Policies                          |
|---------------------------|------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------|------------------------------------------|
| **Definition**            | Predefined policies created and managed by AWS. | Policies created and managed by the customer. | Policies embedded directly into a user, group, or role. |
| **Management**            | Managed by AWS; automatically updated by AWS to include new services and actions. | Managed by the customer; allows for customization to meet specific needs. | Managed directly within the user, group, or role it is attached to. |
| **Reusability**           | Can be attached to multiple users, groups, or roles across the account. | Can be reused across multiple users, groups, or roles within the account. | Specific to a single user, group, or role and cannot be reused. |
| **Modification**          | Cannot be modified by customers.         | Can be created, updated, and deleted by customers. | Can be edited directly within the user, group, or role it is attached to. |
| **Examples**              | `AdministratorAccess`, `AmazonS3ReadOnlyAccess` | Custom policies created by the customer. | Inline policies specific to a particular entity. |
| **Use Case**              | Common permissions needed across many AWS accounts. | Specific needs not covered by AWS managed policies. | Specific, non-reusable permissions tailored to individual users, groups, or roles. |
| **Complexity**            | Simple to use; AWS handles updates and maintenance. | Requires customer to manage and update as needed. | Simple to assign but can become complex to manage at scale. |
| **Policy Scope**          | Broad and generic to cover common use cases. | Can be tailored to precise needs and security requirements. | Very specific to the needs of a single entity. |
| **Best Practices**        | Use for general permissions where AWS managed policies suffice. | Use for more granular control where specific permissions are needed. | Use sparingly for specific cases where managed policies are not suitable. |



**How to create an IAM POLICY** 

Two Format of creating a customer managed policy 

`Visual and  Json format `

**Steps to create an IAM policy** 

+ search IAM and click on policy you will see a list of iam policies 
+ At Top right click on create policy 
+ Step 1 is for you to specify permissions. There are two ways you can specify or setup the permissions . for policy editor you can use  `Visual or Json format` select the service which you wish to grant the user the permission to access 
+ Each service in aws has unique actions that a user can take 
+ After you select the service you will see a list of actions that the user can perform on that service . You can select all actions or specific actions
+ After you select the actions you can specify the resources that the user can access . 

`example of am IAM POLICY `


{
    "Version": "2012-10-17",
    "Statement": [
        {
            "Sid": "VisualEditor0",
            "Effect": "Allow",
            "Action": [
                "ec2:GetActiveVpnTunnelStatus",
                "ec2:StartInstances",
                "ec2:StopInstances"
            ],
            "Resource": "*"
        },
        {
            "Sid": "VisualEditor1",
            "Effect": "Allow",
            "Action": [
                "ec2:DescribeVolumeStatus",
                "ec2:DescribeInstances",
                "ec2:DescribeVolumes",
                "ec2:DescribeVolumesModifications"
            ],
            "Resource": "*"
        }
    ]
}

**Lets Understand The Json Document**

+ `Version` : This specifies the version of the policy language being used. The current version is "2012-10-17".
 It doesn’t represent the date your policy was created; it’s just a format version.

2. `Statement` : A policy can have one or more statements and each statement describe a specific permission 
first statement on our example starts with `"Statement":` and the second statement in our policy starts with `"Sid": "VisualEditor1",` (sid is a statement identifier )
This is an array of individual statements that define the permissions. Each statement includes the following elements:
+ `Sid` (Statement ID): An optional identifier for the statement, used for organizational purposes.
+ `Effect`: Specifies whether the statement allows or denies access. In this example, both statements have an "Allow" effect, meaning they grant permissions.
+ `Action`: Lists the specific actions that are allowed or denied. In this example, the first statement allows actions related to starting and stopping EC2 instances, while the second statement allows actions related to describing EC2 resources.Action simply defines what the user can do 
+ `Resource`: Specifies the AWS resources to which the actions apply. In this example, the resource is set to "*", which means the permissions apply to all resources . You can specify specific ARNs (Amazon Resource Names) to limit the permissions to certain resources if you wish . 

+ `Condition` :  

This is an optional element that allows you to specify conditions under which the policy statement applies. Conditions can be based on factors such as the time of day, the source IP address, or the presence of specific tags on resources. In our example policy, there are no conditions specified. Conditions in IAM policies let you apply extra restrictions beyond just the Action and Resource. Think of them as “if clauses” that narrow when permissions apply (based on tags, IP addresses, time, MFA, encryption, etc.).

`example of a condition in a policy can be` : 
a user must have MFA ( Multi Factor Authenitcation) setup before they can access a resource 


e.g

{
    
    "Sid": "StartStopWithMFA",
    "Effect": "Allow",
    "Action": [
        "ec2:StartInstances",
        "ec2:StopInstances"
    ],
    "Resource": "arn:aws:ec2:ca-central-1:891377304437:instance/i-0930308ce32674ab2",
    "Condition": {
        "Bool": {
            "aws:MultiFactorAuthPresent": "true"
        }
    }
}


`WHY IS AN `ARN` important when setting up an IAM policy`  ? 

ARN stands for Amazon Resource Name, and it is a unique identifier used to specify AWS resources in IAM policies. When setting up an IAM policy, ARNs are important because they allow you to precisely define which AWS resources the policy applies to. By using ARNs, you can grant or deny permissions for specific resources, ensuring that users only have access to the resources they need to perform their tasks while adhering to the principle of least privilege.


3. **IAM ROLES** 

What is an IAM role : 

An IAM role in AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) is an AWS identity with specific permissions that can be assumed by trusted entities,who are these trusted entities , they are ; IAM users, applications, or AWS services. Unlike an IAM user, a role does not have long-term credentials (password or access keys) associated with it. Instead, when a trusted entity assumes a role, it receives temporary security credentials that allow it to perform actions defined by the role's permissions.

**Analogy** 

AN iam role is a like a temporary badge you wear to do a job e.g When you enter a secure building , a security guard gives you a temporary badge , you can use the badge to access specific areas in the building , but you must return the badge once you are done . which is just like an IAM role . an iam role grants temporary permission to a user , group ,or aws services (known as entity) to have access to specific resources within the aws account when carryout a task and will stop using the temporary permission when the task is done and if they were reuse the iam role again aws rotates the access credentials which makes it safe

+ IAM USER - Employee with permanent badge who can have access to some areas in the building 
+ ROLE : visitor badge , that you can borrow or given by security guard to return it later 

**Use Cases for IAM Roles**

+ `Cross-Account Access` : IAM roles can be used to grant access to resources in one AWS account to users or services in another AWS account without sharing long-term credentials.
+ `AWS Service Access` : IAM roles can be assumed by AWS services to perform actions on
behalf of the user. For example, an EC2 instance can assume an IAM role to access S3 buckets or DynamoDB tables without needing to store credentials on the instance.

+ `Federated Access` : IAM roles can be used to allow users from an external identity
provider (such as Active Directory, Google, or Facebook) to access AWS resources without needing to create IAM users for each external user.

+ `Temporary Elevated Permissions` : IAM roles can be used to grant temporary elevated permissions to users or services for specific tasks, such as performing administrative actions or accessing sensitive data, without giving them permanent access.

+ `Automated Workflows` : IAM roles can be used in automated workflows, such as AWS Lambda functions or AWS Step Functions, to allow these services to access resources securely without hardcoding credentials.




IAM ---> username and ( password ( AWS CONSOLE) / Access Keys (CLI)  )  - >
`Authentication` password and access keys   <---> `Authorization` ( attached IAM policies ) -- Long term credentials 

IAM ROLE does not have permanent credentials instead with IAM role you `assume` it  aws gives you a temporary credentials 

`IAM ROLES HAVE 3 PARTS`

1. `Trust Policy` : This defines who can assume the role. It specifies the trusted entities (users, groups, or AWS services) that are allowed to assume the role.


2. `Permission Policy` : This defines what actions you can perform when using the role ( in our anology where can you access in the building using the badge ) e.g Read/Write Access to an s3 bucket - we attached this permission policies to the role.These are Managed Policies (aws managed and customer managed policies )

3. `Session Duration` : This defines how long the temporary credentials obtained by assuming the role will remain valid. The session duration can be set from a few minutes to several hours, depending on the use case and security requirements. When you assume the role ,AWS Issues a temporary security credentials valid for a limited time ( 15 mins up to 12 hours )



***ASSUME ROLE PROCESS** 

You (or a service) sends a request to AWS STS ( `Security Token Service`) ----> STS gives you a temporary keys --> You use the keys to interact with aws services until they expire

`Note` : There are some services you will create on aws that comes with an IAM ROLE


**AM ROLE TRUST ENTITIES**

`Trust Entity` : who we will like to setup to assume the iam role 

+ AWS service  e.g ec2 , s3 , lambda etc 
+ AWS account   
+ Web Identity 
+ Saml 2.0 Federation  
+ Custom Trust Entity 



1. Sign in to the AWS Management Console and open the IAM console at https://console.aws.amazon.com/iam/.
2. In the navigation pane, click on "Roles" and then click on the "Create
role button 
3. Select the type of trusted entity that will assume the role. For example, if you want to create a role for an AWS service, select "AWS service" and choose the specific service from the list.
4. Click "Next: Permissions" to proceed to the permissions page.
5. On the "Attach permissions policies" page, you can choose to attach existing AWS managed policies, customer managed policies, or create a new policy to attach to the role. Select the appropriate policies based on the permissions you want to grant to the role and click "Next: Tags".
6. (Optional) Add tags to the role for easier identification and management, then click "Next: Review".
7. Review the role details, including the trusted entities and attached policies. If everything looks correct , enter a name for the role and an optional description, then click "Create role".


`Trust Policy` : This policy defines who can assume / use the role . In the trust policy the service that assumes the role is what we call the `principal`

`example of a trust policy`

{
    "Version": "2012-10-17",
    "Statement": [
        {
            "Effect": "Allow",
            "Principal": {
                "Service": "ec2.amazonaws.com"
            },
            "Action": "sts:AssumeRole"
        }
    ]
}

``Interview Question : What is the difference between a Trust Policy an IAM policy ? `

+ A Trust Policy defines who can assume an IAM role, specifying the trusted entities (users, groups, or AWS services) that are allowed to use the role. It focuses on the "who" aspect of access control.
+ An IAM Policy, on the other hand, defines what actions are allowed or denied for a
user, group, or role. It specifies the permissions associated with the entity, focusing on the "what" aspect of access control.

iam policies have no principals wheareas trust policies in iam role has principals 

"Action": "sts:AssumeRole" : Permission to use the role 



| Feature               | IAM Role                                                                 | IAM Policy                                                                 |
|-----------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Identity**          | An IAM role is an AWS identity with specific permissions.                | An IAM policy is a JSON document that defines permissions.                |
| **Usage**             | Intended for use by applications, AWS services, or users needing temporary access. | Policies are attached to IAM identities (users, groups, roles) to grant permissions. |
| **Trust Policy**      | Has a trust policy that defines who can assume /use the role.                 | N/A                                                                       |
| **Credentials**       | Provides temporary security credentials when assumed.                     | N/A                                                                       |
| **Attachment**        | Roles are not attached to users but assumed by entities needing the role. | Policies can be attached to users, groups, or roles.                      |                              |
| **Permissions**       | Permissions are defined by the role's attached policies.                 | Defines what actions are allowed or denied on specified resources.        |

4. **IAM Account settings**
+ This is where you can manage your password policy for your aws account
+ You can also manage your access keys here ( rotate , delete etc )

`Under Account Settings we have the following :` 

+ `Password Policy` : This is where you can set the password requirements for your IAM users, such as minimum length, required character types, and whether to allow users to change their own passwords.

+ `Centralized root access for member accounts` ;

This is a feature that allows you to manage root user access for member accounts in an AWS Organizations setup. It provides a centralized way to control and monitor root user access across multiple accounts.  

+ `Security Token Service (STS)`
AWS Security Token Service (STS) is a web service that enables you to request temporary, limited-privilege credentials for AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) users or for users that you authenticate (federated users). STS is used in scenarios where you want to grant temporary access to AWS resources without sharing long-term credentials. ( Session Tokens from the STS endpoints , Outbound Identity Federation)


5. **Identity providers**
+ This is where you can manage your identity providers for your aws account
+ Identity providers allow you to use external identity sources (such as Active Directory, Google, or   
Facebook) to authenticate users and grant them access to AWS resources without needing to create IAM users for each external user. This is often used in scenarios where you want to enable single sign-on (SSO) for your AWS environment, allowing users to log in with their existing credentials from an external identity provider.


B. ACCESS REPORT 

+ This is where you can view the access report for your aws account
+ The access report provides information about the permissions that are being used in your account, including which users, groups, and roles are using which permissions, and how those permissions are being used. This can help you identify potential security risks and ensure that your permissions are being used appropriately. Under Access reports in IAM we have the following features 

1. `Access Analyzer `:

+ This is a feature that helps you identify resources in your AWS account that are shared with an external entity. It analyzes resource-based policies to determine if any resources are accessible from outside your account and provides findings to help you understand and manage those access permissions.It focuses to analyze two important key things which are `External Access and Unused access `

`external access`: Analyzes external entities having access to your aws resources 
`unused access` : This are access that have not been active for a number of days . 


+ `Resource Analysis`: It identify the resources shared with external entities 
+ `Policy validation`: It checks your policies to ensure you are not granting unintended access & it provides recommendations to enhanced your security posture in your aws account  
+ `Continous Monitoring` : Monitors changes in your resource policies & updates the findings  
+ `Displays detailed findings` : it displays information about the external entity , the resource which helps you to understand the level of access the external entity has . 



## Benefits of Using Access Analyzer

+ It Improves security by helping you to identify any access with external entities  and helps you to mitigate or prevent any unintended access to your aws resources 
+ It helps you to identify any unintended resource sharing 
+ It ensures that your resource policies are correctly configured and do not expose your resources by giving you recommendations 
+ It helps in compliance , it provides information or insights into access configurations (SOC2)
+ It generates IAM policies based on Access activity 


2. `Credential Report`: 

+ This is a report that provides information about the credentials (passwords, access keys, MFA devices) associated with your IAM users. It includes details such as when the credentials were last used, whether they are active or inactive, and whether they have been rotated. The credential report can help you identify potential security risks, such as unused or compromised credentials, and take appropriate actions to secure your AWS environment.

**What is MFA ?**:

MFA stands for Multi-Factor Authentication. It is a security mechanism that requires users to provide two or more forms of authentication to verify their identity when accessing a system or service. In the context of AWS,  `MFA adds an extra layer of security` to your AWS account by requiring users to provide a second form of authentication (in addition to their password) when signing in to the AWS Management Console or making API calls. This helps protect your account from unauthorized access, even if a user's password is compromised.

**How to setup MFA for your AWS root Accout** :

1. Sign in to the AWS Management Console using your root account credentials.
2. In the navigation bar, click on your account name (or the account ID) and select "My Security
Credentials" from the dropdown menu.
3. Scroll down to the "Multi-Factor Authentication (MFA)" section and click on
the "Activate MFA" button.
4. Choose the type of MFA device you want to use (e.g., virtual MFA device
or hardware MFA device) and follow the prompts to set up the MFA device.
5. Once the MFA device is set up, you will be prompted to enter the MFA code
generated by your MFA device to complete the activation process.

see more command below on the table 

### User Management Commands

| Command | Description | Use Case |
|---------|-------------|----------|
| `aws iam create-user --user-name MyUser` | Create a new IAM user | Create a new IAM user named `MyUser`. |
| `aws iam delete-user --user-name MyUser` | Delete an IAM user | Delete the IAM user named `MyUser`. |
| `aws iam list-users` | List all IAM users | List all IAM users in the account. |
| `aws iam update-user --user-name MyUser --new-user-name NewUserName` | Update an IAM user's name | Rename an existing user from `MyUser` to `NewUserName`. |
| `aws iam get-user --user-name MyUser` | Get details for an IAM user | Retrieve details for a specific user named `MyUser`. |
| `aws iam create-login-profile --user-name MyUser --password MyPassword` | Create a login profile for an IAM user | Create a password for the IAM user `MyUser` to allow console access. |

### Group Management Commands

| Command | Description | Use Case |
|---------|-------------|----------|
| `aws iam create-group --group-name MyGroup` | Create a new IAM group | Create a new IAM group named `MyGroup`. |
| `aws iam delete-group --group-name MyGroup` | Delete an IAM group | Delete the IAM group named `MyGroup`. |
| `aws iam list-groups` | List all IAM groups | List all IAM groups in the account. |
| `aws iam add-user-to-group --user-name MyUser --group-name MyGroup` | Add a user to a group | Add the user `MyUser` to the group `MyGroup`. |
| `aws iam remove-user-from-group --user-name MyUser --group-name MyGroup` | Remove a user from a group | Remove the user `MyUser` from the group `MyGroup`. |

### Role Management Commands

| Command | Description | Use Case |
|---------|-------------|----------|
| `aws iam create-role --role-name MyRole --assume-role-policy-document file://policy.json` | Create a new IAM role | Create a new IAM role named `MyRole` with the specified trust policy. |
| `aws iam delete-role --role-name MyRole` | Delete an IAM role | Delete the IAM role named `MyRole`. |
| `aws iam list-roles` | List all IAM roles | List all IAM roles in the account. |
| `aws iam attach-role-policy --role-name MyRole --policy-arn arn:aws:iam::aws:policy/MyPolicy` | Attach a policy to a role | Attach a managed policy to the role `MyRole`. |
| `aws iam detach-role-policy --role-name MyRole --policy-arn arn:aws:iam::aws:policy/MyPolicy` | Detach a policy from a role | Detach a managed policy from the role `MyRole`. |

### Policy Management Commands

| Command | Description | Use Case |
|---------|-------------|----------|
| `aws iam create-policy --policy-name MyPolicy --policy-document file://policy.json` | Create a new IAM policy | Create a new IAM policy named `MyPolicy` with the specified policy document. |
| `aws iam delete-policy --policy-arn arn:aws:iam::account-id:policy/MyPolicy` | Delete an IAM policy | Delete the IAM policy named `MyPolicy`. |
| `aws iam list-policies` | List all IAM policies | List all IAM policies in the account. |
| `aws iam attach-user-policy --user-name MyUser --policy-arn arn:aws:iam::aws:policy/MyPolicy` | Attach a policy to a user | Attach a managed policy to the user `MyUser`. |
| `aws iam detach-user-policy --user-name MyUser --policy-arn arn:aws:iam::aws:policy/MyPolicy` | Detach a policy from a user | Detach a managed policy from the user `MyUser`. |

### MFA Management Commands

| Command | Description | Use Case |
|---------|-------------|----------|
| `aws iam create-virtual-mfa-device --virtual-mfa-device-name MyMFADevice` | Create a new virtual MFA device | Create a new virtual MFA device. |
| `aws iam enable-mfa-device --user-name MyUser --serial-number arn:aws:iam::account-id:mfa/MyMFADevice --authentication-code-1 123456 --authentication-code-2 654321` | Enable an MFA device for a user | Enable the MFA device for the user `MyUser`. |
| `aws iam deactivate-mfa-device --user-name MyUser --serial-number arn:aws:iam::account-id:mfa/MyMFADevice` | Deactivate an MFA device for a user | Deactivate the MFA device for the user `MyUser`. |
| `aws iam delete-virtual-mfa-device --serial-number arn:aws:iam::account-id:mfa/MyMFADevice` | Delete a virtual MFA device | Delete a virtual MFA device. |

### Credential Report Commands

| Command | Description | Use Case |
|---------|-------------|----------|
| `aws iam generate-credential-report` | Generate a credential report | Generate a report detailing the status of user credentials. |
| `aws iam get-credential-report --output text --query Content | base64 -d > credential_report.csv` | Retrieve the credential report | Retrieve and save the credential report in CSV format. |


**IAM BEST PRACTICES** 

+ `Avoid using the root account (user)` : The AWS root user has unlimited access—it should never be used for daily tasks. Set up more security for the root user - MFA , use root user only billing or initial setup 

+ `Enforced Strong Password Policies` : Avoid using aws default password policy 
+ `Regularly Rotate IAM user access keys and passwords within (90 days )`
+ `Follow the Principle least previllage Access` . Users and roles should only have the permissions they absolutely need . Avoid using * (wild) card and use arn for specific resources 
+ `Use as much as possible to manage users using Groups rather than managing them individually`
+ Use IAM roles instead of IAM users whenever possible . Assign permissions via roles, not long-lived credentials. E.G EC2 - attach an iam role instead of configure access keys on server . 
+ Audit IAM users and Policies regularly - credential reports , Access analyzer , check maually using aws console 
+ Use as much as customer managed policies over aws managed policies in production environments 
+ Avoid using inline policies as much as possible - use more reusable policies like customer managed policies 
+ Apply conditions to IAM policies 
+ Enable Logging and Monitoring aws cloudtrial ., enable config rules 





**AMAZON EC2 INSTANCES ** 

`What is an EC2 Instance ?` ;

`Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2)` is a web service that provides resizable compute capacity in the cloud. It allows you to run virtual servers, known as instances, in the AWS cloud. EC2 instances can be used for a wide range of applications, from hosting websites and running applications to performing data analysis and machine learning tasks. With EC2, you can quickly scale your compute resources up or down as needed, paying only for the resources you use. 

EC2 in aws is classified under ?
+ Compute service in aws
+ It is a virtual server in the cloud

EC2 - Its one of the Core / Main  services in AWS 
EC2 - Regional Service / Zonal Service 
EC2 instance is the general name of how aws identifies its Compute - its virtual machines. 



**Why do companies need an ec2 service ? or what are some of the cases for an ec2-instance ?** 

+ `Hosting Websites and Web Applications`: EC2 instances can be used to host websites and web applications, providing the necessary compute resources to handle traffic and run backend processes.

+ `Running Applications`: EC2 instances can run a wide variety of applications, including databases, analytics tools, and custom software, allowing businesses to leverage the cloud for their computing needs.

+ `Data Analysis and Machine Learning`: EC2 instances can be used for data analysis and machine learning tasks, providing the necessary compute power to process large datasets and train machine learning models.

+ `Development and Testing`: EC2 instances can be used for development and testing purposes, allowing developers to create and test applications in a scalable and flexible environment.

+ `Batch Processing`: EC2 instances can be used for batch processing tasks, such as running large-scale computations or processing data in parallel.

+ `Disaster Recovery and Backup`: EC2 instances can be used for disaster recovery and backup purposes, allowing businesses to quickly recover from failures and ensure data availability.


`Interview Question :` 

1. Today our cloud cost has grown . how have you helped your company to optimize or reduce cost in the cloud ? 


**PURCHASING OPTION**

1 `On-Demand Instances`: Pay for compute capacity by the hour or second (depending on the instance type) with no long-term commitments. This option is ideal for applications with unpredictable workloads that cannot be interrupted.no upfront payment.You pay as you go and its flexible.This is purchasing option is expensive when compared to other purchasing options in aws . 


`Note` : Many companies uses this pricing Model 

`How On-Demand Pricing Works`

Billing  : Per second (Linux) or per hour (some OS like Windows , Linux ).
Costs depend on  : `Instance type` , `Region` , `OS (Linux, Windows, Red Hat)`. , `Hardware (CPU, memory, GPU)`.

**Advantages**

+ `No upfront costs`: Pay only for the compute capacity you use, with no long-term commitments.
+ `Flexibility`: Scale your compute resources up or down as needed, without worrying about long-term contracts.
+ `Ideal for unpredictable workloads`: On-Demand Instances are perfect for applications with variable or unpredictable workloads
+ `No commitment`: You can stop or terminate the instance at any time without penalty.

**Disadvantages** 

+ Higher cost: On-Demand Instances are generally more expensive than other purchasing options, such as Reserved Instances or Spot Instances, especially for long-term workloads.
+ Not ideal for steady-state workloads: If you have predictable, steady-state workloads, On-Demand Instances may not be the most cost-effective option compared to Reserved Instances or Savings Plans.


2. `Reserved Instances`: Make a one-time payment for a reserved capacity of compute resources for a specified term (1 or 3 years) and receive a significant discount compared to On-Demand pricing. This option is good for applications with steady-state workloads that can commit to using the same instance type over a long period of time. You can choose between three payment options: All Upfront, Partial Upfront, and No Upfront.


`How Reserve instancs Work ?`

The user commits to : 

+ Instance type ( T2 micro ,medium etc )
+ Region - ca-central-1 or us-east-1 etc 
+ Term - How long will you use the instance 1 year to 3 years 
+ Paymant option - Upfront full or Partial , installaments 

AWS applies the Reserve instance discount automatically to running matching instances


`Note:` A Reserved instance is not a physical instance —it’s a billing discount applied to On-Demand instances that match its criteria.
`Note` : You can convert on demand instance purchasing option to reserved instance option 

**Advantages of Reserved Instances** 

+ `Cost savings`: Reserved Instances offer significant discounts compared to On-Demand pricing, especially for long-term workloads.
+ `Predictable costs`: With Reserved Instances, you can predict your compute costs over the term of the reservation, making it easier to budget and plan for your cloud expenses.
+ `Capacity reservation`: Reserved Instances can provide capacity reservation in specific Availability Zones, ensuring that you
have access to the compute resources you need when you need them.
+ `Flexible payment options`: You can choose from different payment options (All Upfront, Partial Upfront, No Upfront) to suit your budget and cash flow needs.

**Disadvantages of Reserved Instances**

+ `Long-term commitment`: Reserved Instances require a commitment of 1 or 3 years, which may not be suitable for all workloads or businesses.
+ `Less flexibility`: If your workload changes or you need to switch `instance types`, you may not be able to take full advantage of your Reserved Instances, leading to potential cost inefficiencies.




3. `Spot Instances`: Bid for unused EC2 capacity at a significantly lower price than On-Demand instances. This option is ideal for applications that can tolerate interruptions and are flexible in terms of when they run. Spot Instances can be interrupted by AWS with a two-minute warning if the capacity is needed for On-Demand or Reserved Instances increase.

+ However even though its cost savings , cheaper the instance can be interrupted by AWS with a two minute warning when the capacity is needed for on demand instances . 
+ Spot Instances let you purchase unused AWS capacity at up to 90% discount compared to On-Demand.

Analogy: It’s like buying leftover airline seats at huge discounts, but you might get bumped off if a full-paying passenger comes along.

## USE CASE : Temporary task , applications that are flexible , fault tolerence , handle interruptions ,data analysis . 

**What Advantages of SPOT INSTANCES**

+ `Cost savings`: Spot Instances can offer significant cost savings compared to On-Demand pricing, making them an attractive option for cost-conscious users.
+ `Scalability`: Spot Instances can be used to quickly scale up your compute resources when
you need them, allowing you to handle spikes in demand without incurring high costs.
+ `Ideal for flexible workloads`: Spot Instances are perfect for applications that can tolerate interruptions and are
flexible in terms of when they run, such as batch processing, data analysis, and testing.
+ `Integration with AWS services`: Spot Instances can be easily integrated with other AWS services, such as Auto Scaling and Amazon EMR, to create cost-effective and scalable solutions.

**Disadvantages of Spot Instances**

+ `Interruption risk`: Spot Instances can be interrupted by AWS with a two-minute warning if
the capacity is needed for On-Demand or Reserved Instances, which can lead to potential disruptions in your applications.
+ `Not suitable for critical workloads`: Spot Instances may not be the best choice for applications that
require high availability or cannot tolerate interruptions, such as production workloads or applications with strict uptime requirements.

`How Spot Instances Work`

+ You request a Spot Instance.
+ AWS checks if spare capacity is available.
+ Instance runs until AWS reclaims it when capacity is on demand 
+ If capacity is needed elsewhere, AWS sends a 2-minute termination notice.

`Note` Spot isn’t about “cheap servers only” — it’s about designing apps that survive interruptions gracefully.

`Spot Pricing Model`

+ Dynamic pricing → based on AWS supply/demand.
+ You set a maximum price (your “bid”).
+ If Spot price goes above your bid → instance is reclaimed.

In practice: AWS keeps prices stable now, but interruptions still happen.


4. `SAVING PLANS` :

Saving Plans is a flexible pricing model that offers significant cost savings compared to On-Demand pricing in exchange for a commitment to use a specific amount of compute resources (measured in $/hour) for a 1 or 3-year term. Saving Plans provide the same discounts as `Reserved Instances` but with more flexibility, allowing you to switch between `instance types`, `regions`, and even AWS services while still benefiting from the discounted rates.

With the saving plans it offers lower price on EC2 instances regardless of the instance family (instance type) , size , OS , tenancy , aws region in exhange the above commitment of usage.

`Note` : Instead of committing to a specific instance (like Reserved Instances), you commit to a dollar amount of usage per hour.


`Example:`

Commitment: $5/hour for 1 year.
Actual usage: $7/hour.
You pay: $5 at discounted rate + $2 at On-Demand


summary using analogy 

`Analogy `

+ On-Demand = buying groceries per item.
+ Reserved Instances = signing a contract for one type of grocery each week. ( plaintains)
+ Savings Plans = a prepaid meal plan where you choose what to eat, but you’ve already committed to spending a certain amount.


5. `DEDICATED HOST`: 

A Dedicated Host is a physical server with EC2 instance capacity fully dedicated to your use. This option is ideal for applications that require a high level of isolation, compliance, or licensing requirements that cannot be met with shared infrastructure. With Dedicated Hosts, you have more control over the placement of your instances and can use your existing server-bound software licenses.

Use Case : scenario where you what EC2 instances to be physically isolated at the host hardware level from instances that belong to other AWS accounts.When a company is subject meet certain policies or regulatory .

`Pricing` : varies based on instance family , region and payment option you choose . You can pay to own the entire host , its expensive whereas dedicated instances which are cost effective

`Why Use Dedicated Hosts?`

+ `Compliance`: Needed for regulations that require workloads to run on dedicated hardware (HIPAA, PCI DSS, FedRAMP).
+ `Licensing`: Lets you use existing server-bound software licenses (Windows Server, SQL Server, Oracle) tied to sockets, cores, or physical servers.
+ You can see the physical sockets, cores, and host IDs for tracking.


6. `DEDICATED INSTANCE` : 

A Dedicated Instance is an EC2 instance that runs on hardware dedicated to a single customer. `Unlike Dedicated Hosts, you do not have control over the physical server`, but your instances are still isolated at the `hardware level` from instances of other customers. Dedicated Instances are ideal for applications that require a high level of isolation but do not have specific compliance or licensing requirements that necessitate a Dedicated Host.

**Advantages of Dedicated Instance:**

+ `Isolation`: Runs on hardware that is dedicated to a single customer, providing a higher level of isolation compared to shared instances.
+ `Cost-effective`: Generally more affordable than Dedicated Hosts while still providing hardware-level isolation.
+ `No need for host management`: You do not have to manage the underlying physical server,as AWS handles the infrastructure, allowing you to focus on your applications.
+ `Ideal for applications with isolation needs`: Suitable for applications that require isolation but do not have specific compliance or licensing requirements that necessitate a Dedicated Host.


**Disadvantages of Dedicated Instance:**
+ `Less control`: You do not have control over the physical server, which may not meet certain compliance or licensing requirements.
+ `Higher cost than shared instances`: Dedicated Instances are more expensive than standard On-Demand instances


`ANALOGY` : 

`Shared EC2` = You rent an apartment in a big building with strangers. ( on demand ) (multi-tenant )
`Dedicated Instance` = You rent an apartment in a building where only your company lives, but you don’t own the whole building.
`Dedicated Host` = You buy the entire building and manage who lives in which room.( single tenant )


Take note 

`Single-Tenant` : → No other customer shares the physical hardware.
`AWS-controlled placement`: You don’t see sockets/cores like in Dedicated Hosts.


7. `CAPACITY RESERVATIONS`: 

A Capacity Reservation is a reservation of EC2 instance capacity in a specific Availability Zone for any duration. This option is ideal for applications that require guaranteed capacity but do not want to commit to a long-term reservation like Reserved Instances or Savings Plans. With Capacity Reservations, you can ensure that you have access to the compute resources you need when you need them, without the long-term commitment of other purchasing options.
GPU 

`Focus`: Capacity assurance (not discounts).


`Analogy:`

On-Demand = you walk into a restaurant and hope there’s a table.
Capacity Reservation = you book a table ahead, even if you don’t eat, you still pay.


**Summary**

Flexibility : On demand instances - Commonly used by most companies 
Predictability , Long term usage : Reserved instances and Saving plans
High cost savings and flexible workloads : Spot instances
Compliance and License requirements : Dedicated Hosts or Dedicated instances
Guaranteed Capacity : Capacity reservations



**Instance Types** :

An instance type is focused on hardware configurations of the computer (ec2 instance ) - Perfomancing 

+ CPU (vCPUs)
+ Memory (RAM)
+ Storage type (EBS volumbes)
+ Network Perfomance 


`Analogy` : Think of Instance Types like car models: some are small and efficient, others are heavy-duty trucks, others are race cars.

Apple --refers to all its computers as Mac ---> different macs e.g Macbook Neo , Macbook air , Macbook pro , Imac , Mac mini , Mac Studio etc 

aws -refers to all its computers as EC2-instances --> has different instance types like t2 ( t2-micro , t2-small , t2-large) ,t3 , t4 etc 

**Note** ; When selecting a specific instance you have to keep in mind the type of workload you want to carryout with the instance . 

**AWS has categorized the instances based on use cases** 

1. `General Purposes` : 

+ These are the most commonly used instance types in AWS. They provide a balance of `compute, memory, and networking resources, making them suitable for a wide range of applications. Examples include the T3, T4g, M5, and M6g instance types.

General Purpose (A/M/T families)

+ Balanced CPU, memory, networking.
+ Best for: web servers, app servers, dev/test.
+ Example: t3.micro (free tier), m5.large.

2. `Compute Optimized (C family)`:
+ These instances are designed for compute-intensive workloads that require high-performance processors. They are ideal or good for applications such as high-performance web servers, scientific modeling, and batch processing. Examples include the C5 and C6g instance types.

Compute Optimized (C family)
+ High CPU-to-memory ratio.
+ Example: c5.large.

3. `Memory Optimized R , X , Z family`:
+ These instances are designed for memory-intensive workloads that require large amounts of RAM. They are ideal
for applications such as in-memory databases, real-time big data analytics, and high-performance computing. Examples include the R5 and R6g instance types.

Memory Optimized (R family)

4. `Storaged Optimized I/D/H Family)`: 

+ These instances are designed for workloads that require high, sequential read and write access to large data sets on local storage. They are ideal for applications such as data warehousing, Hadoop distributed computing, and log processing. Examples include the I3 and I4i instance types.

+ High disk throughput (NVMe SSDs).
+ Best for: Big Data, NoSQL DBs, Hadoop clusters.
+ Example: i3.large.

5. `Accelerated Computing (P/G/F family)`:
+ These instances are designed for workloads that require hardware accelerators, such as `GPUs or FPGAs`. They are ideal for applications such as machine learning, high-performance computing, and video rendering. Examples include the P3 and G4 instance types.

+ GPUs, FPGAs, ML/AI chips.
+ Best for: Machine Learning, Graphics Rendering, AI model training.
+ Example: p3.8xlarge, g5.xlarge, inf1.6xlarge.

6. `High Perfomance Instances` : High-performance computing instances are purpose built to offer the best price performance for running HPC workloads at scale on AWS. These instances are ideal for applications that benefit from high-performance processors, such as large, complex simulations and deep learning workloads.e.g (HPC)
HPC = High Perfomance Compute 

reference : https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AWSEC2/latest/UserGuide/compute-optimized-instances.html


**AMI = Amazon Machine Image**

`What is an AMI ?` ; 

An Amazon Machine Image (AMI) is a `pre-configured` template that contains the necessary information to launch an EC2 instance. It includes the operating system or  application server, or  applications required to run your instance. AMIs can be created from existing instances or provided by AWS as standard images for various operating systems and software configurations. 

`AMI includes :` 

+ An operating system (e.g., Linux, Windows)
+ Application server (e.g., Apache, Nginx , git )
+ Applications (e.g., WordPress, MySQL)
+ Custom Configurations ( security agents , monitoring tools etc )


**WHAT YOU SHOULD KNOW WHEN IT COMES TO AMI** 

+ Template for your ec2 instance. This is the Pre-configured OS or application for your virtual machine
+ You can use AWS provided AMIs or create your own custom AMIs.
+ Users can customize AMI to match their specific needs and they can also modify existing AMI
+ AMIs are region-specific, so you need to ensure that you are using the correct AMI for the region where you want to launch your EC2 instance.
+ You can use a single AMI to create multiple ec2- instances with the same AMI configurations
+ AMIs can be shared with other AWS accounts, allowing you to collaborate and share resources across accounts.
+ There are Private AMI (only the user who owns it can see and use it ) and Public AMI (anyone on the internet (aws) can see and use it)
+ You can also use AWS Marketplace to find and purchase AMIs that are pre-configured with specific software or applications.
+ AMI can be build from a snapshoot
+ AMI can be used to create an Auto Scaling group, which allows you to automatically launch and terminate EC2 instances based on demand.


**TYPES OF AMI**


+ `AWS Marketplace AMIs`: These are pre-configured AMIs provided by AWS and its partners, available in the AWS Marketplace. They include a wide range of software and applications, such as operating systems, databases, and development tools.

+ `Custom AMIs`: These are AMIs that you create yourself, based on your specific requirements. You can customize the operating system, applications, and configurations to meet your needs.

+ `Community AMIs`: These are AMIs that are shared by other AWS users. They can be public or private, and they may include a variety of configurations and software.

+ `AWS-provided AMIs`: These are basic AMIs provided by AWS that include popular operating systems like `Amazon Linux, Ubuntu, and Windows Server`. They serve as a starting point for creating custom AMIs.
