**Target Groups** :

`What is a target group ?`:

A target group is a logical grouping of resources (such as EC2 instances, IP addresses, or Lambda functions) that a load balancer can route traffic to. In AWS, target groups are used in conjunction with load balancers to define the backend resources that will receive traffic from the load balancer. Each target group has a specific protocol and port configuration, and it can be associated with one or more load balancers. The load balancer uses health checks to monitor the status of the targets in the group and routes traffic only to healthy targets. Target groups provide flexibility in managing and scaling your applications by allowing you to easily add or remove resources without affecting the overall architecture of your application.

Different Target Types we have in AWS ?: 

+ `Instance Target Type`: This target type allows you to register EC2 instances as targets for your load balancer. The load balancer will route traffic to the registered instances based on the specified protocol and port configuration.

+ `IP Target Type`: This target type allows you to register IP addresses as targets for your load balancer. This is useful when you want to route traffic to resources that are not EC2 instances, such as on-premises servers or containers running outside of AWS.

+ `Lambda Target Type`: This target type allows you to register AWS Lambda functions as targets for your load balancer. This is useful for serverless applications where you want to route traffic directly to Lambda functions without the need for EC2 instances.

+ `Application Load Balancer (ALB) Target Type`: This target type is specific to Application Load Balancers and allows you to register targets based on the application layer (Layer 7) routing rules. It supports routing based on URL paths, hostnames, and HTTP headers.


`Note` : You can add and remove instances from targets 



**DIFFERENCE BETWEEN APPLICATION LOADBALANCER AND NETWORK LOADBALANCER**


| Feature                | Application Load Balancer (ALB)                    | Network Load Balancer (NLB)                  |
|-------------------------|---------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------|
| **OSI Layer**           | Layer 7 (Application layer)                       | Layer 4 (Transport layer)                    |
| **Supported Protocols** | HTTP, HTTPS, WebSockets                           | TCP, UDP, TLS                                |
| **Routing**             | Path-based & Host-based routing                   | IP + Port based only (no content inspection) |
| **Performance**         | Slightly higher latency due to packet inspection  | Ultra low latency, handles millions of req/s |
| **SSL/TLS**             | Can terminate SSL/TLS at the load balancer        | TLS passthrough (end-to-end encryption)      |
| **IP Addressing**       | Uses DNS name (no static IP)                      | Supports static IPs & Elastic IPs            |
| **Health Checks**       | Application-level (HTTP codes, URL checks)        | TCP-level connectivity (port open/closed)    |
| **Best For**            | Web apps, APIs, Microservices                     | Gaming, VoIP, Financial apps, Streaming apps |
| **Example Use Case**    | E-commerce site with `/checkout` and `/products`  | Real-time trading system or multiplayer game |



**sticky session in loadbalancers**
`What is a sticky session ?`:

A sticky session, also known as `session affinity`, is a feature of load balancers that allows them to route a client's requests to the same backend server (EC2 instance) for the duration of a session (time). This is achieved by using `cookies or other mechanisms to track the client's session` and ensure that subsequent requests from the same client are directed to the same server. Sticky sessions can be beneficial for applications that require session persistence, such as e-commerce websites or applications that maintain user state. However, they can also lead to uneven load distribution if certain servers become overloaded with sticky sessions, so it's important to use this feature judiciously based on the specific requirements of your application.




**How Sticky Sessions Work**

+ *Session Identification*: The load balancer identifies and tracks client sessions, usually by using a session cookie, a URL parameter, or another method.

+ *Consistent Routing*: Once a client (end user or customer) is assigned to a backend server, subsequent requests from the same client are routed to the same server for the duration of the session.

+ *Session Data*: This is particularly useful for applications that store session data locally on the server. Without sticky sessions, the client’s requests might be distributed across different servers, which could lead to inconsistent experiences if session data is not shared or synchronized.


**Common Methods for Sticky Sessions**

+ *Cookie-Based Sticky Sessions*: The load balancer inserts a special cookie into the client’s browser. This cookie identifies the server to which the client is assigned. On subsequent requests, the client sends the cookie back to the load balancer, which uses it to route traffic to the same server.

+ *IP Hashing*: The load balancer uses the client’s IP address to determine which server to route the request to. This method is simpler but less flexible and can lead to issues if clients share IP addresses or change IPs frequently.

+ *URL Parameter*: A unique identifier is included in the URL of each request, and the load balancer uses this identifier to route the request to the appropriate server.


**WHY SHOULD WE USE STICKY SESSIONS IN LOADBALANCING ?**  

1. `Stateful Applications` (An application with a database): Ensures that clients interact with the same server throughout their session, which is essential for applications that maintain state or session-specific information on the server.

2. `Improved Performance` : Reduces the overhead of reloading session data from a shared store or database, which can improve application performance and responsiveness.

3. `Consistency`: Provides a consistent user experience by ensuring that all requests within a session are handled by the same server.



`How to setup sticky session` 

+ Click on Target Groups and under target groups select the target you want 
+ Under attributes click on edit attributes 
+ Under `Stickiness` select enable and choose the duration of the cookie ( e.g 300 seconds )

**AWS Load Balancer Routing Rules**
`What is a Load Balancer routing rule :`

A load balancer routing rule is a `set of conditions and actions` that determine how `incoming traffic` is directed to backend servers (targets) based on specific criteria. These rules allow you to configure the behavior of your load balancer to route traffic in a way that best suits your application's needs. For example, you can create routing rules that direct traffic based on URL paths, hostnames, HTTP headers, or query string parameters. This enables you to implement advanced routing strategies, such as directing requests for different parts of your application to different backend services or instances, improving performance and scalability.

A routing rule determines how the load balancer forwards traffic to target groups (EC2, ECS, Lambda, etc.).

**Types of Routing Rules**

+ Path-based routing 
+ Host-based routing 
+ Query string or http header 
+ http request method 
+ Sourceip 

1. `Host-based routing ( Host Header)`: 

This routing rule directs traffic based on the hostname specified in the HTTP request header. For example, you can route requests for `shop.example.com` to one target group and requests for `blog.example.com` to another target group.
This is useful when hosting multiple services or applications on the same load balancer but under different subdomains or hostnames. 

`Why it’s important`: Many companies want to run multiple apps on the same load balancer but separate them by domain.
and this can be cheaper . 



2. `Path Based Routing`:
This routing rule directs traffic based on the URL path of the incoming request. For example, you can route requests for `/api/*` to one target group and requests for `/user/*` to another target group. This allows you to route different types of requests to different backend services based on the structure of the URL.

This is particularly useful in microservices architectures where different services are exposed under different paths. For example:

Requests to /api might be routed to a target group running your API services.
Requests to /admin might be routed to a target group handling administrative functions.

`Why it’s important`: Lets you split a single domain into different backend services.

`Use case`: Microservices architecture where /login/* goes to an authentication service, /images/* goes to an image CDN (content delivery network) service, and /api/* goes to your backend API pods in EKS.



**How to setup**

Setup:

AWS Application Load Balancer (ALB):
Navigate to ALB in the AWS Console:

+ Go to the AWS Management Console and open the EC2 service.
+ Under "Load Balancing," select "Load Balancers."
+ Choose your Application Load Balancer from the list.
+ Configure the Listener:

Select the "Listeners" tab and click on "View/Edit rules" for the listener you want to configure (usually the HTTP or HTTPS listener).
Add a Rule for Path-based Routing:

+ Click on the "+" button to add a new rule.
+ Choose "Add condition" and select "Path" from the dropdown menu.
+ Enter the path pattern you want to match (e.g., /api/* for API requests or /admin/* for admin requests).
+ Add another condition if needed (e.g., combining path with HTTP methods).

Under the "Then" section, choose the action "Forward to" and select the appropriate target group for the matched path.
Repeat the process for each path you want to route to different target groups.
Save the Rules:

Save the configuration and ensure the listener rules are in the correct order since ALB processes rules in a top-down manner.


                Internet
                    |
                    |
         Application Load Balancer
                    |
                    |
              One EC2 Instance
                 Apache
          -------------------
          |        |        |
         /      /shop     /api


3. `Query String`: 

This routing rule directs traffic based on specific query string parameters in the URL. For example, you can route requests that contain `?type=mobile` to one target group and requests that contain `?type=desktop` to another target group. This allows you to route traffic based on user preferences or device types.

+ Content delivery networks (CDNs) can route requests based on content types (e.g.,
?type=image or ?type=video).
+ A/B testing can be implemented by routing traffic based on query parameters (e.g., ?
version=A or ?version=B).

4. `HTTP Header`:

Routing based on HTTP request headers is useful when you need to direct traffic based on specific header values. For instance, you might want to route traffic differently based on the user's location, device type, or other metadata passed in the headers.


**Examples & Use Cases**

`Device-specific routing:`

1. `User-Agent`: iPhone → Send to a lightweight mobile backend.
  User-Agent: Chrome → Send to full web backend.

2. `Geo or region-based routing`:

Header X-Region: EU → Route to EU-compliant servers.
Header X-Region: US → Route to US servers.

3. `Internal vs External traffic`:

+ Header X-Internal-Client: true → Send to internal admin dashboards.
+ Otherwise → Send to public services.

**How to setup HTTP HEADER**

+ Go to your Application Load Balancer settings.
+ Under "Listeners," edit the rules for your listener.
+ Add a condition using "HTTP header."
+ Specify the header name (e.g., User-Agent) and the value you want to match (e.g., mobile).
+ Assign the request to the appropriate target group based on the header condition.
+ Save your changes.

5. **HTTP request method**

The ALB can inspect the HTTP method (GET, POST, PUT, DELETE, etc.) in the request and decide which target group to forward it to.

`Why It’s Important`

+ Some workloads are read-heavy (GET requests) while others are write-heavy (POST/PUT/DELETE).Separating them allows you to scale independently, secure differently, or send writes to more powerful servers (EC2).

6. **Source IP–Based Routing**

Routes traffic based on the client’s IP address or CIDR (Classless interdomain routing ) range.

`Why It’s Important`

+ Security: You can separate internal users (office IPs) from external customers.
+ Compliance: Route traffic from certain regions (e.g., EU) to data centers that meet local laws.





**HEALTH CHECKS IN LOAD BALANCERS**

This is way a load balancer checks your backend services to ensure that they are ready to receive traffic before it routes the traffic to them it uses the ping mechanism to check your backend servers in the (target groups) to make sure they are working properly before it routes traffic to it.

`Understanding Load balancers health checks`

`Health Check Configurations`

`Health Check protocol ;`
+ Health Checks are configured at the target group level within the load balancer
+ You can specify the protocol type e.g HTTP , HTTPS , TCP

+ `Health Check Path` : This is the specific endpoint path or url that your load balancer pings to determined weather the target (ec2 instance) server or application target is heathy and is able to handle or receive a request  e.g `/` : this is the default path and it uses the root (/) to test but you can set your own custom path e.g /health /healthcheck depending on configurations 


Advance settings 

+ `Health Check Port`:
This is the port number that your load balancer uses to perform health checks on the targets. It can be the same as the application port or a different port specifically designated for health checks. For example, if your application listens on port 80, you can configure the health check to use port 80 as well, or you can choose a different port (e.g., 8080) if you have a separate endpoint for health checks.
 
`Healthy Threshold` : 
The number of consecutive health checks successes required before considering an unhealthy target healthy.

`UnHealthy threshold`: This is the number of times your load balancer is going to ping your backend servers (targets) before it determines or mark the target as unhealthy .

`Timeout` : this is the period of time your load balancer will wait for a response before it marks the target as unhealthy


+ `Interval` : 
The approximate amount of time between health checks of an individual target
This is the time period between each health check ping. For example, if you set the interval to 30 seconds, the load balancer will ping the target every 30 seconds to check its health status.

+ `Success code `:

This is the HTTP status code that your load balancer expects to receive from the target in response to a health check ping. For example, if you set the success code to 200, the load balancer will consider the target healthy if it receives an HTTP 200 OK response from the target during a health check. 
other status codes 404,  405 issues 


**NOTE**: TCP does not use status codes instead it uses connection states : e.g 

+ `Open`: The load balancer successfully established a TCP connection to the target.
+ `Closed`: The load balancer attempted to establish a TCP connection but it was closed by the
target or did not respond within the timeout period.
+ `Error`: An error occurred while trying to establish a TCP connection to the target (e
.g., network issues, target not reachable).





**Trust store**:

A trust store is a repository that contains trusted certificates, usually public certificates of Certificate Authorities (CAs), that are used to verify the authenticity of the certificates presented by remote servers or clients.

Purpose / Use case : The trust store ensures that only certificates signed by trusted CAs(Certificate Authority) are accepted, thereby establishing a secure connection between the client and the server.



`Load Balancer Access Logs` : 

Access logs provide detailed information about the traffic that is handled by your load balancer. They include data such as the time of the request, the client's IP address, the request path, the response status code, and more. Access logs are useful for monitoring and analyzing traffic patterns, troubleshooting issues, and gaining insights into how users are interacting with your application.


`AWS Loadbalancer X-Ray` :

AWS X-Ray is a service that helps developers analyze and debug distributed applications, such as those built using microservices architecture. It provides insights into the performance of applications by tracing requests as they travel through various components, including load balancers, backend services, databases, and external APIs. With AWS X-Ray, you can identify bottlenecks (issues), understand latency issues, and gain visibility into the overall health of your application. It allows you to see how requests are processed across different services and helps you pinpoint where errors or performance issues may be occurring in your application stack.



**AWS-specific best practices when setting up a load balancer** 

1. `Choose the Right Load Balancer Type by Picking` it based on traffic type and application requirements.

2. `High Availability & Redundancy` ; Always enable cross-zone load balancing so traffic distributes evenly across AZs,Register targets in multiple AZs for resilience.Use Route 53 health checks + failover if you need multi-region resiliency.

3. `Security Best Practices`: 

+ Terminate TLS/SSL at the load balancer using AWS Certificate Manager (ACM)
and ensure that the certificate matches the dns name for your application to avoid untrusted domain errors 
+ Redirect all HTTP traffic to HTTPS (enforce encryption in transit).
+ Restrict inbound access via security groups (only required ports, e.g., ,80 ,443) should be allowed 
+ Use AWS WAF on ALBs for protection against common web exploits.
+ Enable Access Logs to S3 for auditing and compliance.



4. `Monitoring & Observability`: 

+ Enable CloudWatch metrics (latency, 4xx/5xx errors, request count).
+ Configure ALB/NLB access logs to track request patterns.
+ Use AWS X-Ray to trace requests through the LB to backend services.
+ Send LB logs to S3 with lifecycle policies (e.g., delete after 90 days).

5. `Target Groups & Health Checks`

+ Use ALB listener rules for routing by host/path instead of running multiple LBs.
+ Fine-tune health check settings (path, interval, threshold) to avoid false positives or negatives.
+ Remove unhealthy instances automatically from rotation.


6. `Performance & Optimization`
+ Consider sticky sessions (session affinity) only if absolutely required, since it can create uneven load distribution.
+ For high throughput, prefer to use  NLB (millions of requests/second).
+ Use ALB listener rules for routing by host/path instead of running multiple LBs.




**AUTO SCALING GROUP (EC2-INSTANCE )**

What is an `Auto Scaling Group` (ASG)

An Auto Scaling Group (ASG) in AWS is a service that automatically adjusts the number of EC2 instances in a group based on defined policies, health checks, and schedules. It helps maintain application availability and allows you to scale your EC2 capacity up or down according to demand. ASGs can automatically replace unhealthy instances, ensuring that the desired number of instances is always running. This is particularly useful for applications with variable workloads, as it helps optimize costs while maintaining performance and availability.


This is a logical grouping of your ec2-instances managed by aws to scale in and out when there is demand. In your auto scaling group you can setup Minimum , maximum and desired number of instances in the group . With this setup you want to make sure the number of instances running can handle the load ,so the auto scaling group will scale up and down to handle incoming request and traffic 


**Benefits of an Auto-scaling Group or auto scaling**

( High availability , cost optimization , performance , flexibity etc )
1. `High Availability` :
ASGs automatically replace unhealthy instances, ensuring that your application remains available even if some instances fail. This helps maintain a consistent user experience and reduces downtime.
+ You can automatically scale without any manual intervention which reduces operational overhead .

2. `Cost Optimization` :
ASGs allow you to scale your EC2 capacity up or down based on demand, which helps optimize costs. You only pay for the instances you need, and you can avoid over-provisioning or under-provisioning resources.It helps in reducing cost because without an auto-scaling you will provisioned the number of serves ahead of time and you can over provisioned which will cost you more money but with auto scaling you only pay for what you need .

3. `scalability` : 
ASGs can automatically adjust the number of instances based on defined policies, such as CPU utilization or request count. This allows your application to handle varying levels of traffic and ensures that you have enough resources to meet demand during peak times while scaling down during low traffic periods.


4. `Performance` :
ASGs can automatically adjust the number of instances based on defined policies, such as CPU utilization or request count. This helps maintain optimal performance during traffic spikes or periods of low demand.

5. `Flexibility` :
ASGs can be configured with various scaling policies, such as target tracking, step scaling, or scheduled scaling. This allows you to customize how your application scales based on specific metrics or time-based schedules.

5. `Integration with Load Balancers` :
ASGs can be integrated with Elastic Load Balancers (ELBs) to distribute incoming traffic across the instances in the group. This ensures that traffic is balanced and that new instances are automatically added to the load balancer when they are launched.

6. `Health Checks` :
ASGs perform health checks on the instances in the group. If an instance is deemed unhealthy, it can be automatically terminated and replaced with a new instance, ensuring that your application remains healthy and responsive.

7. `Easy Management` :
ASGs provide a simple way to manage the lifecycle of your EC2 instances. You can easily update the launch configuration, change scaling policies, or adjust the desired capacity without having to manually intervene in the instance management process.

8. Auto scaling has the a feature call instance refresh and With Instance Refresh, you can roll out patched AMIs automatically across the fleet.Works well with AWS Config, CloudTrail, and IAM roles to ensure only the right instances run with the right permissions.




**To create an Auto Scaling Group you need the following** 

What is a `Launch Template` : 

A Launch Template in AWS is a resource that contains the configuration information required to launch EC2 instances. It serves as a blueprint for creating instances and includes details such as the Amazon Machine Image (AMI) ID, instance type, key pair, security groups, and other settings. Launch Templates allow you to define and reuse instance configurations, making it easier to launch multiple instances with consistent settings. They are commonly used in conjunction with Auto Scaling Groups to ensure that new instances launched by the ASG have the desired configuration. By using Launch Templates, you can simplify the process of launching instances and maintain consistency across your EC2 fleet.

**What are some of the Advantages of using a Launch Template**

1. `Consistency` : Launch Templates ensure that all instances launched from the template have the same configuration, which helps maintain consistency across your EC2
fleet. This is particularly important when you need to launch multiple instances with the same settings, such as in an Auto Scaling Group.
2. `Versioning` : Launch Templates support versioning, allowing you to create multiple versions of a template with different configurations. This enables you to easily manage changes to your instance configurations over time and roll back to previous versions if needed.
3. `Integration with Auto Scaling Groups` : Launch Templates can be used directly with Auto Scaling Groups, making it easier to define the configuration for instances that will be automatically launched and terminated based on scaling policies.
4. `Simplified Instance Launching` : Using a Launch Template simplifies the process of launching
instances, as you can specify all the necessary configuration details in one place. This reduces the chances of errors and ensures that instances are launched with the correct settings.
5. `Flexibility` : Launch Templates allow you to specify a wide range of configuration options
for your instances, including AMI ID, instance type, key pair, security groups, user data, and more. This flexibility enables you to tailor the instance configuration to meet the specific needs of your application.
6. `Cost Management` : By using Launch Templates, you can easily manage and optimize costs
associated with launching instances. You can create different versions of the template with varying instance types or configurations to test and optimize for cost efficiency.
7. `Automation` : Launch Templates can be used in automated workflows, such as with AWS
CloudFormation or AWS CLI, to streamline the process of launching instances as part of larger infrastructure deployments or updates. This helps improve efficiency and reduces manual intervention in instance management.


Dont specify the subnet when creating a template for an auto scaling group template this is because when you are creating your auto scaling group you will need to specify the availability zone and subnets at the time of creation. instance is in a public subnet : aws recognizes template based on the subnet



`AMI `
An Amazon Machine Image (AMI) is a pre-configured virtual machine image that contains the necessary information to launch an EC2 instance. It includes the operating system, application server, and any additional software or configurations needed to run your application. AMIs can be created from existing instances or provided by AWS, and they serve as a template for launching new instances with the same configuration. By using AMIs, you can quickly and easily deploy instances with consistent settings, which is particularly useful when scaling applications or creating multiple instances for high availability.


HANDS 

HOW TO CREATE AN AUTO-SCALING GROUP 

1. Create a launch template with the desired configuration (AMI, instance type, security groups, etc.).
2. Go to the Auto Scaling Groups section in the AWS Management Console.
3. Click "Create Auto Scaling Group" and follow the prompts to set up the group.
4. Select the launch template you created in step 1.
5. Select the network (VPC)
6. Choose the availability zones and subnets for your instances.
7. Configure availability zone distribution . we have Balanced best effort and Balanced Only 

+ **Balanced best efffort* : 

If our autoscaling fails in one availability zone , auto scaling will attempt to launch in another availability zone 

+ **Balanced Only* : 

Auto scaling will only attempt to launch in the availability zones you specified and if it fails in one availability zone it will not attempt to launch in another availability zone it will continue it attempts on the unhealthy availability zone 

8. Intergrate auto scaling with other services in our case we will integrate with a loadbalancer. 
+ Select create a new load balancer  
+ Select the load balancer type (application load balancer or network load balancer).
+ Configure the load balancer settings (listeners, security groups, etc.).
+ allow auto-scaling to create a new target group

9. Configure Group Size and Scaling 
+ Set the desired capacity (initial number of instances) that will run in the auto scaling group 
+ Set the minimum capacity (the minimum number of instances that should always be running)
+ Set the maximum capacity (the maximum number of instances that can be launched) on your behalf by the auto scaling group 

10. Configure Scaling Policies

+ No Scaling Policy ( Auto scaling will not add or remove instances )

+ Target Tracking Scaling Policy (e.g., maintain average CPU utilization at 50%)

11. Select Instance Maintenance Policy 

+ Mixed Behaviour ( No Policy ): 

If an instance becomes unhealthy, the auto scaling group will terminate it and launch a new instance to replace it. This is the default behavior and helps maintain the desired capacity of the group.
The auto scaling might create a new instance before terminating the old one and it can just simply terminate the instance before creating a new one 


+ Prioritized Availability ( Launch before terminating ) : 

If an instance becomes unhealthy, the auto scaling group will first launch a new instance before terminating the unhealthy one. This approach helps ensure that there is no downtime during the replacement process, as the new instance will be up and running before the old one is terminated.

+ Control cost ( Terminate before Launch ) : 

If an instance becomes unhealthy, the auto scaling group will first terminate the unhealthy instance before launching a new one. This approach can help control costs by ensuring that you are not running more instances than necessary during the replacement process. However, it may result in a brief period of downtime while the new instance is being launched and initialized.

+ Flexible ( Custom Behaviour ): 

You can create a custom maintenance policy that defines specific actions to take when an instance becomes unhealthy. For example, you could configure the policy to send a notification to administrators, perform additional health checks, or trigger a Lambda function before launching a new instance or terminating the old one. This allows for greater flexibility in handling unhealthy instances based on your specific application requirements and operational preferences.


12 . Additional capacity settings : 

+ Set up Capacity reservation settings by selecting one of the options -- select default 

1. Default 
2. None 
3. Capacity resevation only 
4. Capacity reservation first 

13 : Additional settings
+ Instance scale-in protection : 

This setting allows you to protect specific instances from being terminated during scale-in events. When enabled, instances with scale-in protection will not be automatically terminated by the Auto Scaling Group when scaling down, which can be useful for critical instances that need to remain running even during periods of low demand. You can enable this setting for individual instances within the Auto Scaling Group to ensure that they are not inadvertently terminated during scaling activities.

+ Monitoring 

Enable detailed monitoring to get more granular metrics for your instances, which can help you make informed decisions about scaling policies and understand the performance of your application. Detailed monitoring provides additional insights into CPU utilization, network traffic, disk activity, and other important metrics that can help you optimize your Auto Scaling Group's performance and cost-efficiency.

+ Auto Scaling group deletion protection

This setting prevents the accidental deletion of your Auto Scaling Group. When enabled, it requires an additional confirmation step before the group can be deleted, providing an extra layer of protection against unintended deletions that could lead to downtime or loss of resources. This is particularly important in production environments where the Auto Scaling Group is critical for maintaining application availability and performance. 

You can select any of the following options 

+ None default ( auto scaling can be deleted )
+ Prevent force deletion ( Means auto scaling before deleted all instances inside must be terminated and detached )
+ prevent all deletion : auto scaling group cant be deleted

i highly recommend to select prevent all deletion in production 


13. Set up Notifications 

+ You can configure notifications to be sent to an SNS topic when certain events occur within your Auto Scaling Group, such as instance launches, terminations, or scaling activities. This allows you to stay informed about the status and changes in your Auto Scaling Group and take appropriate actions if needed. You can choose to receive notifications for specific events or all events related to your Auto Scaling Group, depending on your monitoring and operational requirements.




**HOW DOES AUTO SCALING WORK** 

+ Auto scaling monitors your instances. It continously monitor metrics from your ec2 instances or services.When this metric cross predefined thresholds . it triggers the scaling actions e.g cpu is over 80% it should create new instances , it can be an unhealthy instance and it creates a new instance to replace 

+ `Scaling Actions` : Scale up or down . When it scales up that means there is `increase in demand` , new instances are launched and traffic is routed to that instance and when scales down this means `demand has decreased` and it will go back to your desired state(number of instances you want at all time). 

+ `Maintained desired capacity`: Auto Scaling ensures that the number of running instances matches the `desired capacity`, even if some instances fail or become unhealthy. This capacity is defined in the Auto Scaling Group configuration

+ `Health Management`. Auto Scaling performs health checks on the instances in the Auto Scaling Group. If an instance is found to be unhealthy, it is terminated and replaced with a new one to maintain the desired capacity.


**AUTO SCLAING POLICIES**

`What is an Auto scaling policy` : 

An Auto Scaling policy is a set of rules and conditions that define how an Auto Scaling Group should respond to changes in demand or specific metrics. These policies determine when to scale up (add instances) or scale down (remove instances) based on predefined thresholds, such as CPU utilization, request count, or custom metrics. Auto Scaling policies can be configured to maintain optimal performance and cost-efficiency by automatically adjusting the number of instances in the group according to the current workload. There are different types of Auto Scaling policies, including target tracking, step scaling, and scheduled scaling, each offering different ways to manage scaling actions based on your application's needs.

**Types of Auto Scaling Policies**

1. **Dynamic Scaling Policy**` : Dynamic scaling policies uses real time metrics to scale in aws and it gets the metrics from cloudwatch ( we have 3 times of this dynamic scaling policy type  which are `Target Tracking , Step and simple scaling`)

+ `Target Tracking Scaling` : 

This policy type automatically adjusts the number of instances in an Auto Scaling Group to maintain a specified target value for a particular metric, such as average CPU utilization or request count. For example, you can set a target tracking policy to maintain an average CPU utilization of 50%. If the average CPU utilization exceeds 50%, the policy will trigger a scale-up action to add more instances. Conversely, if the average CPU utilization falls below 50%, it will trigger a scale-down action to remove instances. Target tracking policies provide a simple and effective way to maintain optimal performance while minimizing costs by automatically adjusting capacity based on real-time metrics.  

+ Step scaling 
+ Simple scaling 


install stress

sudo apt install stress 
stress --cpu 4

+ `Step Scaling`: 

This policy type allows you to define specific scaling actions based on different thresholds for a particular metric. For example, you can configure a step scaling policy to add 2 instances if CPU utilization exceeds 70% and add an additional 2 instances if it exceeds 90%. Similarly, you can define steps for scaling down when CPU utilization falls below certain thresholds. Step scaling policies provide more granular control over scaling actions when compared to target tracking policies, allowing you to specify different responses based on varying levels of demand. 

+ `Simple Scaling`: Adds or removes a fixed number of instances based on a single scaling action.e.g cpu more than 80% you can choose how many instances it should add .

This policy type allows you to define a single scaling action that is triggered when a specific metric crosses a defined threshold. For example, you can set a simple scaling policy to add 1 instance when CPU utilization exceeds 80% and remove 1 instance when it falls below 20%. Simple scaling policies are straightforward and easy to configure, making them suitable for basic scaling needs where you want a consistent response to changes in demand without the need for multiple thresholds or steps.


2. **Predictive Scaling Policy** : 

Predictive scaling policies use machine learning algorithms to analyze historical data and predict future traffic patterns. Based on these predictions, the policy can proactively scale your Auto Scaling Group up or down before the actual demand changes occur. This allows you to maintain optimal performance and cost-efficiency by ensuring that you have the right number of instances running at all times, even during unexpected spikes in traffic. Predictive scaling is particularly useful for applications with variable workloads or seasonal traffic patterns, as it helps you stay ahead of demand and avoid performance issues caused by under-provisioning or over-provisioning resources.

3. **Scheduled Action Policy**:

Scheduled action policies allow you to define scaling actions that occur at specific times or on a recurring schedule. For example, you can set a scheduled action to scale up your Auto Scaling Group every weekday at 9 AM and scale down at 5 PM. This is particularly useful for applications with predictable traffic patterns, such as business hours or seasonal spikes, allowing you to ensure that you have the appropriate number of instances running during peak times while minimizing costs during off-peak periods. Scheduled action policies provide a simple way to automate scaling based on time-based patterns without the need for real-time metrics or predictions.


`What is an Auto scaling cool down period `:

A cool down period in Auto Scaling is a specified amount of time that must pass after a scaling activity before another scaling activity can occur. This period allows the system to stabilize after a scaling action, preventing rapid fluctuations in the number of instances due to temporary spikes or drops in demand. During the cool down period, the Auto Scaling Group will not initiate any new scaling actions, even if the metrics indicate that scaling is needed. This helps ensure that the scaling process is more controlled and prevents unnecessary scaling activities that could lead to increased costs or performance issues. The cool down period can be configured based on your application's needs and typical traffic patterns.




**ROUTE 53** 

`What is Route 53`: ?

Amazon Route 53 is a scalable and highly available `Domain Name System (DNS)` web service provided by AWS. It is designed to route end-user requests to applications hosted on AWS or outside of AWS. Route 53 translates human-readable domain names (like www.example.com) into IP addresses that computers use to connect to each other. It also offers features such as `health checks, traffic routing policies, and domain registration, making it a comprehensive solution for managing DNS and directing traffic to your applications efficiently and reliably. 

Route 53 is an `AWS service` that you use to manage your DNS (Domain Name System) and you can use it for `DNS management , Traffic management ,domain registration , health checks and DNS security` . 

**Key Features of Route 53**

1. `DNS Management` : Route 53 allows you to manage your domain names and their associated DNS records, such as A records, CNAME records, MX records, and more. You can create and manage these records to route traffic to your applications hosted on AWS or elsewhere.

+ This allows you to perform DNS queries globally and manage domains on route 53
+ Supports public hosted zones (for internet-facing apps) and private hosted zones (for internal AWS networks).
+ Fully integrated with AWS resources like EC2, ALB, CloudFront, and S3 using Alias records (unique to AWS).

2. `Traffic Management` : Route 53 provides various traffic routing policies to help you manage how traffic is directed to your applications. These policies include:

+ `Simple routing`: Routes traffic to a single resource.
+ `Weighted routing`: Distributes traffic across multiple resources based on specified weights.
+ `Latency-based routing`: Routes traffic to the resource with the lowest latency for the user.
+ `Failover routing`: Routes traffic to a secondary resource if the primary resource is unhealthy.
+ `Geolocation routing`: Routes traffic based on the geographic location of the user.
+ `Geoproximity routing`: Routes traffic based on the geographic location of the user and the
resources, allowing for biasing towards certain resources.
+ `Multi-value answer routing`: Returns multiple healthy resources in response to DNS queries, allowing for client-side load balancing.

3. `Domain Registration` : Route 53 allows you to register new domain names directly through the AWS Management Console. You can search for available domain names, register them, and manage your domain settings all in one place.
+ When you register a domain ,Route 53 automatically creates a public hosted zone when you register a domain.
+ Supports top-level domains (TLDs) like .com, .net, .org, .ca, etc.
+ You can renew, transfer, or manage WHOIS contact info all inside the AWS Console.

4. `Health Checks` : Route 53 can monitor the health of your resources (like web servers or endpoints) using health checks. If a resource becomes unhealthy, Route 53 can automatically route traffic away from it to healthy resources based on your routing policies.
+ You can configure health checks to monitor endpoints, such as HTTP, HTTPS, or TCP.
+ Health checks can be associated with DNS records to enable failover routing.
+ You can set up CloudWatch alarms based on health check status for proactive monitoring.

5. `DNS Security` : Route 53 supports DNSSEC (Domain Name System Security Extensions) to help protect against DNS spoofing and ensure the integrity of your DNS data. With DNSSEC, you can digitally sign your DNS records, allowing clients to verify that the responses they receive are authentic and have not been tampered with.


**Key terminologies**

`What is a DNS in simple words`?

DNS stands for `Domain Name System`. It is like the phone book of the internet that translates  human-friendly domain names (like www.example.com) into IP address that computers use to identify each other on the network. When you type a website address into your browser, DNS is responsible for finding the corresponding IP address so that your browser can connect to the correct server and load the website.


**Domain Names** 

A domain name is a human-readable or human friendly address that is used to access websites and other resources on the internet. It is a unique identifier that maps to an IP address, allowing users to easily remember and access websites without needing to remember complex numerical IP addresses. For example, "www.team4techsolutions.com" is a domain name that can be used to access a website hosted on a server with a specific IP address. Domain names are hierarchical and typically consist of multiple levels, such as top-level domains (TLDs) like .com, .net, or .org, and second-level domains like "example" in "www.example.com". They play a crucial role in the functioning of the internet by providing a user-friendly way to access online resources.

+ In route 53 we can register a new domain 
+ In route 53 we can transfer an existing domain name from another domain registrar like godaddy , namecheap etc 
+ In route 53 when you register your domain and its approved a Public hosted zone is created by default and you will need to create a private hosted zone for internal facing dns records . 

**Domain Registrar** :

A domain registrar is a company or organization that manages the reservation of domain names on the internet. They are accredited by the `Internet Corporation for Assigned Names and Numbers (ICANN`) to sell and manage domain name registrations. Domain registrars provide services such as registering new domain names, renewing existing domain names, transferring domain names from one registrar to another, and managing DNS settings for registered domains. Examples of popular domain registrars include `GoDaddy, Namecheap, Google Domains, and AWS Route 53`. When you register a domain name through a registrar, you typically pay an annual fee to maintain ownership of that domain name.

On aws to register a domian you will have to check if the domain name is available. Once the domain name is available you can purchase or register the domain . AWS will deduct the cost of the domain using the card associated to the aws account . 

Domains have different Classifications or Hierachy 

`Domain Classification `

Our case study will be team4techsolutions.com 

1 `Root Level (Invisible)` : 
This is the highest level of the domain hierarchy, represented by a dot (.) at the end of a domain name. It is not visible in regular domain names but serves as the starting point for the DNS resolution process. example team4techsolutions.com. but in real life the dot (.) is not visible

2. `Top-Level Domain (TLD)`:

We have different types of Top Level Domains.But when we talk about top level domain we are referring to the part of the domain name that ends with dot (.) e.g .com , .org .ca   ---> team4techsolution  (TLD)--> .com . 
There are different types of Top Level Domain (TLD)

+ `Generic or General TLDs (gTLDs)` : These are the most common type of TLDs and include extensions like .com, .org, .net, .info, etc. They are not restricted to any specific purpose and can be registered by anyone for various uses.

+ `Country Code Top Level Domain (ccTLDs)` : These TLDs are specific to countries or territories and consist of two letters, such as .us for the United States, .ca for Canada, .uk for the United Kingdom,ng for Nigeria , cm for Cameroon etc. They are often used by organizations or individuals associated with a particular country.

+ `Sponsored Top Level Domain (sTLDs)` : These TLDs are sponsored by specific organizations or communities and have certain eligibility requirements for registration. Examples include .edu for educational institutions, .gov for government entities, and .mil for military organizations. , ,corp for co-operations etc 

3. `Second-Level Domain (SLD)`:

This is the part of the domain name that is located directly to the left of the top level domain. In other words the SLD is directly below the top level domain and is often used to identify the organization or individual entity that owns the domain e.g team4techsolutions.com is a domain name but team4techsolutions is the second level domain . 
e.g team4techsolutions  .com

4. `Sub Domains`: Anything left after the second level domain (SLD) is the subdomain . In other words These are the extension of a domain name and its used to organized and categorize different sections or services within a domain e.g jenkins.team4techsolutionsinc.com , apps.team4techsolutionsinc.com

or

Subdomains are additional levels of a domain that are created by adding a prefix to the second-level domain. They are used to organize and navigate to different sections or services of a website. For example, in the domain name "blog.team4techsolutions.com", "blog" is a subdomain of the second-level domain "team4techsolutions.com". Subdomains can be used to create separate areas for different purposes, such as "shop.team4techsolutions.com" for an online store or "support.team4techsolutions.com" for customer support. They help improve the structure and organization of a website while still being part of the same overall domain.

**DNS RESOLUTIONS** 
DNS resolution is the process of translating a human-readable domain name (like www.google.com) into an IP address that computers use to identify each other on the network. When you enter a domain name into your web browser, the DNS resolution process begins, and it involves several steps:

1. The browser (Mozilla Firefox , Google Chrome ) checks its local cache to see if it has recently resolved (accessed) the domain name. If it finds a cached entry, it uses that IP address to connect to the server.

2. If the browser does not have a cached entry, it sends a DNS query (DNS request) to the configured DNS resolver (usually provided by your Internet Service Provider or a public DNS service like Google DNS or Cloudflare DNS).

3. The DNS resolver checks its own cache for the requested domain name. If it finds a cached entry, it returns the corresponding IP address to the browser.

4. If the DNS resolver does not have a cached entry, it starts a recursive query process to find the authoritative DNS server for the domain. It first queries the root DNS servers to find out which top-level domain (TLD) server is responsible for the requested domain.

5. The root DNS server responds with the IP address of the TLD DNS server (e.g., for .com domains).

6. The DNS resolver then queries the TLD DNS server to find out which authoritative DNS server is responsible for the second-level domain (e.g., team4techsolutions.com).

7. The TLD DNS server responds with the IP address of the authoritative DNS server for the requested domain.

8. The DNS resolver then queries the authoritative DNS server for the requested domain to get the IP address associated with the domain name.

                                       ▼
                        ┌────────────────────────────┐
                        │ 1. Browser Cache Check      │

                        └────────────────────────────┘

Question 

`When You Copy an EC2 Public IP and Paste It in a Browser — Does It Go Through Route 53?`

Answer

What Actually Happens When You Paste the IP

+ Browser sees a number (not a domain).
+ It skips DNS lookup.
+ It opens a direct connection to that IP using HTTP/HTTPS.
+ The request goes straight to your EC2 instance’s public IP via the internet gateway (if your EC2’s security group and NACL allow inbound access on port 80/443).


**ICAAN : Internet Corporation for Assigned Names and Numbers :**

ICANN is a nonprofit organization responsible for coordinating the global domain name system (DNS) and IP address allocation. It oversees the management of top-level domains (TLDs) and ensures the stable and secure operation of the DNS.Head Quarter is in California USA , Los Angeles .website icann.org 

Functions 

+ Acredits domains registrar 
+ It manages the root DNS zone (.)
+ They coordinate the allocation of ip address spaces
+ Oversees domain name policy development 


**IANA : Internet Assigned Numbers Authority** : This is a branch or function within ICANN responsible global coordination of the DNS root , ip addressing and other protocol resources  and it administers Top level domains including country code TLD , generic top level domain

**DNS RECORDS IN ROUTE 53** 
DNS records are the instructions that tell the DNS how to route traffic for a specific domain or subdomain. In Route 53, you can create various types of DNS records to manage how your domain is accessed and how it interacts with other services. 

when you register a domain , route 53 will automatically create you a  Public hosted zone for that domain and will also  creates 2 important record types which are
: `NS (Name server) record`  & `SOA (start of Authority ) record`.


Some common types of DNS records in Route 53 include:

1. `NS (Name server ). Record` :

This record specifies the authoritative name servers for a domain. When you register a domain, Route 53 automatically creates NS records that point to the Route 53 name servers responsible for managing the DNS records for that domain. These NS records are essential for the proper functioning of your domain, as they tell the DNS system where to look for the authoritative information about your domain.  

+ NS records tell the world which name servers are responsible for managing your DNS records for your domain 
+ Name servers are specialized to answer DNS queries , like "What is the IP address of google.com ? " 

example of NS record type when you create a public hosted zone 

ns-1641.awsdns-13.co.uk.
ns-94.awsdns-11.com.
ns-1137.awsdns-14.org.
ns-965.awsdns-56.net.

2. `SOA (start of Authority ) record`: 

This record contains administrative information about the domain, such as the primary name server, the email address of the domain administrator, and various timing parameters for DNS zone transfers and caching. The SOA record is automatically created when you create a hosted zone in Route 53 and is essential for the proper functioning of your DNS records. It helps ensure that DNS information is propagated correctly across the internet and that changes to your DNS records are properly managed.

+ It contains important information about the domain and how DNS changes for the domain are managed 
+ It identifies the primary DNS server for the domain and includes administrative info like when the DNS was last updated and how dns records should check for updates 

example of SOA record type when you create a public hosted zone
ns-1641.awsdns-13.co.uk. awsdns-hostmaster.amazon.com. 1 7200 900 1209600 86400


3. `A RECORD (Address record)`: 
This record maps a domain name to an IPv4 address. It is one of the most common types of DNS records and is used to direct traffic to a specific server or resource associated with the domain. For example, if you have a web server hosting your website, you would create an A record that points your domain name (e.g., www.team4techsolutions.com) to the IP address of that web server 

+ An A records routes traffic to IPV4 (123.456.4568) and some AWS resources (Load balancer , cloudfront distribution)  example of an ipv4 : `123.456.4568`
www.team4techsolutions   record A ------> route traffic to IPv4 or Loadbalancer 

+ Use case : good when you want to point your domain or sub-domain to specific server e.g EC2 that uses IPV4 address typically for websites 

4. `AAAA RECORD (IPv6 address record)`:

This record maps a domain name to an IPv6 address. It serves the same purpose as an A record but is used for resources that have an `IPv6 address` instead of an IPv4 address. For example, if you have a server that is configured with an IPv6 address, you would create an AAAA record to point your domain name to that IPv6 address.

+ AAAA RECORD routes traffic to ipV6 (`2607:fea8:a5de:1200:90f6:8fa6:ee81:aa7`) and some AWS resources (alias to application loadbalancer , alias app runner , alias to cloud distribution)

+ USE CASE : similar with ipv4 but for websites or services that uses IPV6 instead of IPV4 


5. `CNAME RECORD (`Canonical Name Record`): 

This record maps or route a domain name to another domain name. It is often used to create aliases for a domain, allowing you to point multiple domain names to the same resource. For example, you could create a CNAME record that points "www.team4techsolutions.com" to "team4techsolutionsinc.com", so that both addresses will resolve to the same IP address or resource.

africa.team4techsolutions.com -----------> team4techsolutions.com
canada.team4techsolutions.com -----------> team4techsolutions.com

+ Routes traffic to another domain name or aws resources (alaising) 
+ USE CASE : it can be used when you want multiple domain names to point to a single service , or domain e.g app.team4techsolutions.com ---> points to ---> team4techsolutions.com 


6. `MX RECORD` (Mail Exchange Record) : 

This record specifies the mail servers responsible for receiving email on behalf of a domain. It is used to route email messages to the correct mail server based on the domain name in the email address. For example, if you want to set up email services for your domain, you would create MX records that point to the mail servers that will handle incoming email for your domain. e.g team4techsolutions.com  
calson@team4techsolutions.com 

USE CASE : It can be used when you want to configure email delivery for a domain for instance you set a configuration to direct email traffic for team4techsolutions.com to a mail server.

7. `TXT RECORD`:

This record allows you to associate arbitrary text with a domain name. It is commonly used for various purposes, such as verifying domain ownership, implementing email security measures (like SPF, DKIM, and DMARC), and providing additional information about the domain. For example, you might create a TXT record to verify ownership of your domain with a third-party service or to set up email authentication to prevent spoofing. 


USE CASE : Its used to verify domain ownership , its also used for setting SPF (Sender policy framwork) for emails or it stores other validation or configuration data .

8. `PTR RECORD (POINTER RECORD)`:
This record maps an IP address to a domain name, essentially performing the reverse function of an A record. It is used for reverse DNS lookups, allowing you to determine the domain name associated with a specific IP address. For example, if you have an IP address and want to find out which domain name it corresponds to, you would use a PTR record to perform this lookup.

USE CASE : Its often used to verify the authenticity of servers especially for emails

9. `SRV RECORD ( Service Locator)`:
This record is used to specify the location of services within a domain. It allows you to define the hostname and port number for specific services, such as SIP (Session Initiation Protocol) or XMPP (Extensible Messaging and Presence Protocol). For example, if you have a service running on a specific port and want to make it discoverable through DNS, you would create an SRV record that points to the hostname and port of that service.

USE CASE: Commonly used for services like SIP (Session initiation protocol) and microsoft services that requires port to connect

10. `SPF RECORD `(Sender Policy Framework):

This record is a type of TXT record that is used to specify which `mail servers are authorized to send email on behalf of a domain`. It helps prevent email spoofing by allowing domain owners to define a list of approved mail servers. For example, if you want to specify that only certain mail servers are allowed to send email for your domain, you would create an SPF record that lists those servers.

11. `NAPTR RECORD` (Naming Authority Pointer Record ):

This record is used to specify rules for how to rewrite domain names based on certain criteria. It is often used in conjunction with SRV records to provide additional information about how to route traffic for specific services. For example, if you have a service that requires specific routing based on the type of request, you might use NAPTR records to define those routing rules.

12. `CAA (CERTIFICATE AUTHORITY AUTHORIZATION)`:

This record allows domain owners to specify which certificate authorities (CAs) are authorized to issue SSL/TLS certificates for their domain. It helps enhance security by preventing unauthorized CAs from issuing certificates for your domain. For example, if you want to restrict which CAs can issue certificates for your domain, you would create a CAA record that lists the authorized CAs.

USE CASE : The CAA record helps prevent Bad Certificate Authorities from issuing unauthorized certificates for your domain, increasing security.

13. `DS RECORD ( Delegation signer record )`:

This record is used in DNSSEC (Domain Name System Security Extensions) to establish a chain of trust between a parent zone and a child zone. It contains the hash of the public key used to sign the child zone's DNS records, allowing resolvers to verify the authenticity of the DNS data. For example, if you have a child zone that is signed with DNSSEC, you would create a DS record in the parent zone to link it to the child zone's signing key.


USE CASE : DS records are used in conjunction with DNSSEC to provide cryptographic verification of DNS data, ensuring that the DNS records are genuine and have not been altered by an attacker.





**TIME TO LIVE (TTL)** 

`What is Time to Live (TTL)`? :

Time to Live (TTL) is a value in DNS records that specifies the duration (in seconds) that a DNS resolver should cache the information before it needs to query (send another request) the authoritative DNS server again for updated information. When a DNS record is queried, the resolver stores the response in its cache along with the TTL value. The resolver will continue to use the cached information until the TTL expires, at which point it will need to perform a new DNS query to retrieve fresh data(information)



+ Setting a High Time to Live (TTL) for example 24 hours means less traffic to your route 53 but this can lead to outdated information especially if the website or resource is dynamic .High TLL is good for static websites or websites that the contain does not change very often

+ Setting a Low Time to Live (TTL) for example 5 minutes means more traffic to your route 53 but it ensures that users get the most up-to-date information. Low TTL is good for dynamic websites or websites that the content changes very often like news website or e-commerce website .


Question ? 

`What is the difference between Time to Live and Sticky Session ? `:

+ TTL is how often users will query dns (route 53) to get new information whereas sticky session is how long the dns will resolve with a specific backend server (ec2) application 


**Route 53 Routing policy**

`What is a Routing Policy in Route53` : 

A routing policy in Route 53 is a set of rules that determine how DNS queries `( DNS request)` are routed to different resources based on specific criteria. Routing policies allow you to control the flow of traffic to your applications and services by defining how DNS queries are resolved. They can be used to implement various traffic management strategies, such as load balancing, failover, and geographic routing, to ensure that users are directed to the most appropriate resources based on factors like latency, health, and location.


`TYPES OF ROUTING RULES IN ROUTE 53 `

+ Simple routing 
+ Weighted routing
+ Latency-based routing
+ Failover routing
+ Geolocation routing
+ Geoproximity routing
+ Multi-value answer routing
+ Ip based routing policy 

1. `Simple Routing `:

Simple routing is the most basic type of routing policy in Route 53. It allows you to route traffic to a single resource, such as an EC2 instance, load balancer, or S3 bucket, without any additional logic or criteria. With simple routing, all DNS queries for a specific domain or subdomain will be directed to the same resource, regardless of factors like latency, health, or geographic location. This type of routing is suitable for applications that do not require complex traffic management and can rely on a single endpoint to handle all requests. Simple routing is easy to set up and manage, making it a good choice for straightforward use cases where you want a consistent response to all DNS queries.

Key points 

+ This routes dns queries or traffic to a single resource such as webservers , amazon s3 , cloudfront distribution. 
+ It is the default routing policy when you are creating record sets in route 53 
+ we can specify multiple values (ip addresses) in the same record (a random value is going to be choosen by the client
+ You can also use an alais to route traffic to different endpoints e.g loadbalancer and use simple routing 
+ This routing policy can not be associated with route 53 health check 

USE CASE : You can use it when you want dns queries to be routed to a single resource or EC2 instance . 
example : team4techsolutions.com to route or direct traffic -----> ec2 instance ip address its good for static websites or single instance websites 

2. `Weighted Routing`:

Weighted routing allows you to distribute traffic across multiple resources based on specified weights. With this routing policy, you can assign a weight to each resource, and Route 53 will route traffic to those resources in proportion to their weights. For example, if you have two resources with weights of 70 and 30, Route 53 will route approximately 70% of the traffic to the first resource and 30% to the second resource. This type of routing is useful for load balancing, testing new versions of an application (canary deployments), or gradually shifting traffic from one resource to another.

+ This policy will send all dns queries or request / route the traffic based on assigned weights . You can distribute traffic across multiple resources in proportion of  weight 

+ It controls the percentage of traffic that goes to a single or a specific resource e.g ec2 instance 

+ The weight does not really need to sum up to %100
+ The Record name (DNS name) must be same and the record type must be weighted 
+ Weighted Routing Policy can be associated with Health Checks 
+ When you assigned a weight of 0 (zero) route 53 stops sending any traffic to the resource ( ec2)
+ if you set the weight on all  records to 0 (zero) then all records will return equally 


+ Traffic % : Weight of a specific record 
              ---------------------------
              Sum of the weight of all records 


`USE CASE` : This is useful for testing new versions of your application (A/B testing) or when you want to gradually roll out changes .
e.g You have release a new version of your application and you want to send 70 % of traffic to production environment and 30% of traffic to the new version .

3. `Geo-location Routing policies`: 

Geolocation routing allows you to route traffic based on the geographic location of the user. With this routing policy, you can specify different resources to serve users from different regions or countries. For example, you could route users from North America to one set of servers and users from Europe to another set of servers. This type of routing is useful for improving performance by directing users to resources that are geographically closer to them, as well as for compliance with data sovereignty regulations.

+ It routes traffic based on the geographical location of the user making the request .
+ You can specify a contintent or a specific country 
+ You can associate health checks with geo-location routing policies

USE CASE : This is good when companies want to rollout new versions of their application to specific group of people for test first . 
+ This also helps in serving content based on location for better perfomance 

4. `Latency Routing Policy` :

Latency-based routing allows you to route traffic to the resource that provides the lowest latency for the user. With this routing policy, Route 53 measures the latency between the user's location and the available resources and routes traffic to the resource with the lowest latency. This type of routing is useful for improving performance by directing users to resources that can respond more quickly to their requests.



`what is latency?` : It refers to the time it takes for data to move or travel from its source (location) to (another) its destination and back .

+ When data moves faster from source (where data is coming from) to destination and back its considered low latency or on the other hand (good )
+ when data moves or travel slowly from source to destination and back its considered `high latency` . (bad)
+ When creating Latency routing policies  you need to specify where the resource is located or have been created within your aws account 
+ Latency routing policy routes traffic to resources that provides the lowest latency based on the geographical location of the user.
+ Route 53 measures latency between its `DNS servers` and `your resources` 
+ latency routing policies supports health checks


5. `Failover Routing Policy` :

Failover routing allows you to route traffic to a primary resource under normal conditions and automatically failover to a secondary resource if the primary resource becomes unhealthy. With this routing policy, you can set up health checks to monitor the status of your resources, and if the health check fails for the primary resource, Route 53 will route traffic to the secondary resource. This type of routing is useful for ensuring high availability and minimizing downtime for your applications.

+ This routing policy routes traffic to a specific resource under normal conditions . That is as long as the resource is healthy and available its going to route the traffic to it but if the resource becomes unavailable it will (failover)to the secondary resource you have configured .
+ Route 53 continously monitors health of the primary resource using health checks .

USE CASE : This is good for high availability because of a backup resource in case something happens to the primary. 

6. `Geoproximity Routing Policy`:

Geoproximity routing allows you to route traffic based on the geographic location of both the user and the resources. With this routing policy, you can specify a bias value to influence the routing decisions, directing more traffic to resources that are geographically closer to the user. This type of routing is useful for improving performance by directing users to resources that are not only geographically closer but also have better network conditions.

This routes traffic based on the geographical location of the users and resources and it allows you to adjust traffic distribution based on a bias 
+ You can change the size of the coverage using Bias and a positive bias value that is anything 1+ can increase the size of coverage and anything -0 will shrink the size coverage 


7. `Ip-based Routing Policy`: 

This routing policy allows you to route traffic based on the IP address of the user making the request. With this routing policy, you can specify different resources to serve users from different IP address ranges. This type of routing is useful for controlling access to your resources based on the user's IP address, such as allowing or blocking traffic from specific regions or organizations.

+ It supports health checks 



8. `Multi-value Answer Routing Policy` :

Multi-value answer routing allows you to return multiple values (such as IP addresses) in response to a DNS query. With this routing policy, Route 53 will return multiple healthy resources in response to a DNS query (request), and the client can choose which resource to connect to. This type of routing is useful for improving availability and load balancing by providing multiple options for clients to connect to.

It routes traffic to multiple resources and returns multiple values ( such as ip addresses ) for DNS query.The DNS resolver will return selected value of its choice .

# HEALTH CHECKS IN ROUTE 53 

`What is health check in route53:`

A health check in Route 53 is a monitoring mechanism that allows you to assess the health and availability of your resources, such as web servers, application servers, or other endpoints. Health checks are used in conjunction with routing policies (like failover routing) to determine whether a resource is healthy and can receive traffic. If a resource fails a health check, Route 53 can automatically route traffic to an alternate resource based on the routing policy you have configured. Health checks help ensure high availability and reliability for your applications by allowing you to proactively manage and route traffic based on the health status of your resources.

+ With Route 53 Health Checks, the checkers are publicly accessible, meaning they can only monitor public endpoints — such as public EC2 instances, load balancers, or websites that are reachable over the internet. However, these Route 53 health checkers operate outside of your VPC, so they cannot directly access private resources (like internal EC2 instances, private ALBs, or endpoints within a Private Hosted Zone).

To work around this limitation, you can use Amazon CloudWatch as an intermediary:

+ Create a CloudWatch Metric that tracks the health or status of your private resource (for example, a custom metric that reports app health or instance availability).

+ Set up a CloudWatch Alarm that changes to ALARM state when the metric indicates a problem.

+ Create a Route 53 Health Check that monitors the CloudWatch Alarm instead of the endpoint directly.


`TYPES OF HEALTH CHECKS WE CAN CREATE IN ROUTE53 `

1. `Endpoint health checks` :
This type of health check monitors the health of a specific endpoint, such as a web server or application server. It sends requests to the endpoint at regular intervals and checks for a successful response (e.g., HTTP 200 status code) to determine if the endpoint is healthy or unhealthy.

2. `Calculated health checks` :(This checks the status of other health checks )

This type of health check calculates the overall health status based on the results of multiple endpoint health checks. You can specify how many of the underlying health checks must be healthy for the calculated health check to be considered healthy.

+ A calculated Health check, checks other health checks . It evaluates the health of a resources based on the results of other health checks or multiple health checks .

+ You have the option to set when the parent health check can be deemed healthy . e.g if one out of the 3 or 4 health checks is healthy then the parent health is healthy and vice versa

+ You can use OR , AND , NOT conditions when setting this up
+ You can monitor upto 256 Child Health Checks
+ You can specify how many of the child health checks needs to pass before the parent pass

3. `Cloudwatch Alarm heaLth check ` :

This type of health check monitors the state of a CloudWatch alarm. It checks whether the alarm is in an OK, ALARM, or INSUFFICIENT_DATA state to determine the health status.

Route 53  cloudwatch alarm health check doesnt support high resolution cloudwatch alarms . A high resolution cloudwatch alarm is any alarm that has a less than 1 mins threshold


`ROUTE 53 RESOLVERS` : 

A Route 53 Resolver is a service that provides DNS resolution for resources within a VPC. It allows you to resolve domain names to IP addresses for resources that are hosted within your VPC, such as EC2 instances, RDS databases, and other AWS services. The Route 53 Resolver acts as a DNS server for your VPC, handling DNS queries from your resources and providing responses based on the DNS records you have configured in Route 53. This service is essential for enabling communication between resources within your VPC and ensuring that they can resolve domain names correctly.

By default route 53 resolver allows resources within a vpc to query DNS records . This includes resolving aws servce endpoints ,external domain names and resources in other vpcs .


Resolver endpoints for Hybrid environments-

it provides inbound and outbound endpoints to facilitate DNS queries (request).

( This allows DNS queries from on-premise networks or other vpcs to resolve domian names within a vpc )

`Outbound resolver endpoint` :

Enables DNS queries from a VPC (aws) to reach an on-premise dns server for resolving domain names not hosted in AWS.

USE CASE --> This is when you want aws resources or applications in aws that needs access to resources located in your cooperate data center ( office or physical location )
